Simply-typed constant-domain modal lambda calculus I: distanced beta reduction and combinatory logic Thanks: Thanks to Thomas Ede Zimmermann, Dana Scott, and the reviewers for comments and feedback.
Sean Walsh ORCID:0009-0007-5460-778X

Abstract
. A system 𝝀 θ \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta} is developed that combines modal logic and simply-typed lambda calculus, and that generalizes the system studied by Montague and Gallin. Whereas Montague and Gallin worked with Church’s simple theory of types, the system 𝝀 θ \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta} is developed in the typed base theory most commonly used today, namely the simply-typed lambda calculus. Further, the system 𝝀 θ \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta} is controlled by a parameter θ \theta which allows more options for state types and state variables than is present in Montague and Gallin. A main goal of the paper is to establish some basic metatheory of 𝝀 θ \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta} : (i) an Andrews-like characterization of its models in terms of combinatory logic is given, and this combinatory logic involves a 𝖡𝖢𝖪𝖶 \mathsf{BCKW} -like basis rather than an 𝖲𝖪𝖨 \mathsf{SKI} -like basis and (ii) semantic conservation and expressibility results relating 𝝀 θ \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta} to the maximal system 𝝀 ω \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega} are proven. Similar results are proven for the relation between 𝝀 ω \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega} and 𝝀 \boldsymbol{\lambda} , the corresponding ordinary simply-typed lambda calculus. This answers a question of Zimmermann in the semantics of the simply typed setting. In a companion paper this is extended to Church’s simple theory of types. We further develop a partial correspondence between a pure combinatory logic centered on the 𝖡𝖢𝖪𝖶 \mathsf{BCKW} -like basis and the weak deductive system for 𝝀 ω \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega} wherein β \beta -reduction is not allowed under a lambda abstract, and we use this to show partial deductive conservation between the maximal system 𝝀 ω \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega} and the intermediary systems 𝝀 θ \boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta} .

1.
Introduction

Two of the great achievements of modern logic are modal logic and typed lambda calculus. At the advent of formal semantics in linguistics, Montague developed a system that integrated the two.
1
1

1

Montague’s work
[
55
]
is discussed at length in standard semantics textbooks, such as
[
32
]
,
[
40
]
, and
[
19
]
. Montague’s work was made well-known in part through the work of Partee; see
[
59
]
for some of the history. The theory is sometimes divided into the intensional theory of types and Montague grammar (e.g.
[
40
, Chapters 5-6]
). This paper focuses on the intensional theory of types, as did Chapters 1-2 of Gallin’s book
[
39
]
. In recent decades, textbook treatments of semantics focus foremost on Montague grammar in extensional contexts. That is the topic of
[
45
]
, with its anticipated sequel
[
83
]
being devoted to intensional matters.
However, by contemporary lights, Montague’s theory is both too strong and too weak. It is too strong in that he worked only with Church’s simple theory of types, replete with the resources of quantification and identity.
2
2

2

Church
[
20
]
took quantification as primitive and defined identity; Henkin
[
46
]
did it the other way around, and Henkin’s approach is in e.g. Andrews’ book
[
3
, Chapter 5]
.
But modern typed lambda calculi work with a weaker base system, and have many different extensions besides Church’s simple theory of types.
3
3

3

See
[
8
]
for an authoritative modern treatment of the base system and its extension to intersection types and recursive types. See
[
10
]
,
[
56
]
for lengthy treatises on dependently-typed lambda calculus, including Coquand and Huet’s Calculus of Constructions
[
23
]
,
[
22
]
, which was an important predecessor to the Lean proof verification system (cf.
[
34
]
); a distinct but related branch of dependently-typed lambda calculus is Martin-Löf Type Theory (cf.
[
52
]
). Simply-typed lambda calculus is the internal logic of Cartesian closed categories, and see
[
51
]
for the generalization to topoi. See
[
86
]
,
[
42
]
for systematic contemporary treatments of the denotational and operational semantics for programming languages, developed initially by Scott, Strachey, and Plotkin (
[
69
]
,
[
70
]
,
[
65
]
,
[
66
]
).
Further, Montague’s theory is too weak in that it does not have many of the features of modern modal logics, such as two-dimensionality and actuality operators and other devices for referring to many distinct states, and binding many distinct variables of state type, within one and the same expression.
4
4

4

For two-dimensional semantics and actuality operators, see
[
30
]
,
[
57
]
. For hybrid logics and multiple state variables, see
[
4
]
,
[
24
, Part I]
. In recent joint work with Köpping
[
50
]
, Zimmermann developed an extension of Montague’s original system, in the standard semantics, which contains two-dimensionality and multiple state types.
(Following common usage in modal logic, ‘‘state’’ is a term of art which, depending on application, covers worlds, times, machine-configurations, etc.) A chief aim of this paper is to remedy this deficit, and to begin the development of a thoroughly modern version of Montague’s simply-typed modal lambda calculus. This should be of interest wherever modal logic and simply-typed lambda calculus and related systems are used, be in intensional semantics, in higher-order metaphysics, or in program verification.
5
5

5

For higher-order metaphysics, see
[
85
]
,
[
37
]
. Outside of the operational semantics, two other important paradigms of program verification are propositional modal logic (e.g.
[
21
]
), and dependently-typed lambda calculus (e.g.
[
12
]
).

Another goal of this paper and its companion is to answer one of the outstanding questions about the metatheory of Montague’s original system.
6
6

6

The companion paper is
[
84
]
.
Zimmermann showed in 1989 that Montague’s simply-typed modal lambda calculus was expressively rich in the
standard semantics
, in that its ostensibly more limited vocabulary could express anything expressible in the usual simply-typed lambda calculus formed with an additional atomic type for states.
7
7

7

[
87
]
.
In other words, in the standard semantics, Zimmermann showed that by using simply-typed lambda calculus with an atomic type for possible worlds one cannot really say or assert anything above and beyond what one can say or assert in the object-language of Montague’s modal logic itself, i.e. with ordinary statements of possibility and contingency.
8
8

8

[
87
, p. 75]
. This result is discussed extensively by Zimmermann in
[
88
]
; it is formally stated as the second theorem on
[
88
, p. 31]
. In work with Köpping, Zimmermann extended his result to settings with more than one kind of state type, cf. discussion of their equation (10) in
[
50
, pp. 171, 175-176]
.
But the standard semantics is both incomplete and highly set-theoretically entangled since its validity relation is not recursively enumerable; hence any result about it may well just be a result about the ambient set theory and need not match up with what one can express with primitive rules for the system. Zimmermann asked in 1989 whether his result would generalise to the
Henkin semantics
.
9
9

9

[
87
, §4.2 pp. 75-76]
. For other discussions in the Montagovian tradition friendly to the Henkin semantics, see
[
60
, §3.1.1 p. 316]
,
[
48
, p. 98]
. The distinction between the Henkin semantics and the standard semantics comes up in all discussions of higher-order logic and related systems, see e.g.
[
75
]
,
[
16
]
.
In this and the companion paper, I resolve Zimmermann’s question, largely in the affirmative.
10
10

10

The caveat “largely” is due to the presence of description axioms and constant symbols for description operators. If both are included in the right way, the answer is affirmative. The general situation is more complicated. See
[
84
]
for more details.

The division between the two papers is as follows: in this present paper I focus on the simply-typed lambda calculus, and in the companion paper I extend the results to Church’s simple theory of types (again, the key difference is that the latter includes identity and quantification).

The simply-typed modal lambda calculus is designated with
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, and there are as many of these systems as there are choices of atomic types and choices of signatures. The atomic
state
types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
are not allowed to be the codomain of a functional type; and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is further controlled by the parameter
\theta
which dictates how many variables the state types have (see §§
2.1
-
2.2
for formal definition). Due to the lack of variables, the usual proofs of completeness for
\beta\eta
-equality using open term models are not in general available (cf. Example
3.3
). One special case where it is available is when parameter
\theta
is set to countably infinitely many variables for each state type (the maximal setting), in which case
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is written as
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. The first part of the resolution to Zimmermann’s question in the semantics of the simply-typed setting is the following pair of theorems (proven in §
4.4
):

Theorem 1

(Semantic conservation of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
over
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
)
.

Every model of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is also a model of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. Hence for terms
M,N
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, one has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\models M=N
iff
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\models M=N
.

Theorem 2

(Semantic expressibility of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
)
.

Suppose that term
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
is such that its free variables and constants are those of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Then there is a term
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with the same free variables and constants such that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\models M=N
.

In these theorems, the semantic validity relation
\models M=N
just means sameness of denotation of
M,N
for all models of the relevant system and for all variable assignments. Since
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
is maximal, these theorems also imply semantic conservation and expressibility results between nested intermediary systems of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

The ordinary non-modal simply-typed lambda calculus is designated as
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, and there are as many of these systems as there are choices of atomic types and choices of signatures. Each system of simply-typed modal lambda calculus
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
is equipped with a choice of atomic types, and this then induces a system of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. The types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
are a small subset of the types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
: for instance if
A
is a state type, then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
does not have a type
A\rightarrow A
, but
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has this type (see §
2.3
for formal definition). The second part of the resolution to Zimmermann’s question in the simply-typed setting is the following pair of theorems (proven in §
5.1
):

Theorem 3

(Deductive conservation of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
over
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
)
.

For types
A
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
and terms
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, one has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
iff
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
.

Theorem 4

(Deductive expressibility of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
)
.

Suppose that
A
is a type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
and that term
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is such that its free variables and constants have types in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. Then there is a term
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
with the same free variables and constants such that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
.

In these theorems, the deductive relation
\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
just means
\beta\eta
-equality of
M,N
in the relevant system. By the Completeness Theorem for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and the Completeness Theorem for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
(cf. Theorem
20
), we can also equivalently express conservation and expressibility in terms of model-theoretic validity. Putting these four theorems together, one also has a semantic conservation result for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
over
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and a semantic expressibility result for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Theorems
3
-
4
are proven in §
5
, and the proofs are comparatively short since one can make use of all the known tools of the ordinary simply-typed lambda calculus, like Church-Rosser and strong normalization.

The proofs of Theorems
1
-
2
go through a characterization of Henkin models of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
in terms of combinatory logic. This characterization, proven in §
4.3
, generalizes Andrews’ similar characterization for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. Like Andrews’ result, this characterization gives “the way out of Henkin mysterious conditions that all
\lambda
-terms must have a denotation’’:
11
11

11

This apt praise for Andrews is from Dowek
[
31
, p. 255]
. Andrews’ original result is
[
2
, Proposition 4, Theorem 1 pp. 390-391]
; see
[
8
, Proposition 3.1.19(iii) p. 101]
.

Theorem 5

(Combinatorial characterisation of models)
.

If
\mathcal{M}
is a frame, then
\mathcal{M}
is a model of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
iff the denotations of all the
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
are well-defined.

The combinatory logic used to prove this result deploys a
\mathsf{BCKW}
-basis, to which we add a combinator
\mathsf{D}
, rather than an
\mathsf{SKI}
-basis. See Question
4.2
for whether there is any deeper necessity in this.

A chief difficulty in proving the theorem is that one has to work with a more complicated notion of
\beta
-reduction. Let us denote by
\beta_{0}
the usual beta reduction
\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}\big)N\rightarrow_{\beta_{0}}L[v:=N]
, subject to the usual constraints. Intuitively, it is an expression of how an input
N
gets processed according to a rule
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}
: namely one systematically replaces free instances of
v
in
L
by
N
to form the term
L[v:=N]
. The more general version which seems necessary is
a distanced version of beta reduction

\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N\rightarrow_{\beta}\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}
(which is subjected to natural constraints, cf. Definition
2.6
). This is “distanced” in that the input
N
is separated from the lambda abstract
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}
by the intermediary terms
\vec{M}
. The usual beta reduction
\beta_{0}
is just “distance zero”
\beta
-reduction.
12
12

12

While the reductions
\beta_{0}
and
\beta
are different, we do not know if the equalities are different (cf. Question
2.6
and Proposition
12
).

The main theorems of this paper can be used to produce a new rule which when added to
\beta\eta
-reduction allows
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
to be deductively complete. Namely, for terms
M,N:A
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, say that term
M
reduces to term
N
by
the
\infty
-rule
iff
M
is
\beta\eta
-equal to
N
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
(cf. Definition
4.5
). Then the Completeness Theorem
20
for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
together with the semantic conservation result (Theorem
1
) implies the following, which is proven in §
4.5
:

Theorem 6

(Completeness of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with
\infty
-rule)
.

For terms
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, one has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\beta\eta\infty}M=N
iff
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\models M=N
.

Further one can show that when
\theta
has at least two variables for each state type, then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
has an open term model formed with respect to
\beta\eta\infty
-equality (cf. §
5.2
). The natural open question is whether
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
has a Completeness Theorem for
\beta\eta
-equality, or equivalently whether
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
is conservative over
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
for
\beta\eta
-equality (cf. Question
4.5
). By the methods used to establish Theorem
5
, these two questions are equivalent to the question of whether
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
can prove all of the same
\beta\eta
-equalities made up from applications, variables in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, constants, and
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinators in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

We can establish partial conservation of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
over
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
for
\beta
-equality and
\beta\eta
-equality in a large class of cases. Recall that in the lambda calculus, reduction relations are traditionally taken to be preserved under lambda abstracts: that is, if
M
reduces to
N
, then
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
reduces to
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{N}
; and similarly, equality relations are traditionally taken to be preserved under lambda abstracts (this is called the
\xi
-rule). In so-called weak lambda calculus,
13
13

13

See Çağman-Hindley
[
17
]
for the untyped setting, and Sestini
[
72
]
for the typed setting.
one restricts this rule,
14
14

14

See the outset of §
6
for examples.
and a
w
-prefix is used to indicate that weak reduction and equalities are at issue in the lambda calculus. The weak lambda calculus is of interest because evaluation in many programming languages does not happen under lambda abstracts,
15
15

15

See
[
73
]
,
[
13
]
for surveys and taxonomies of different evaluation strategies, many of which are weak. See
[
29
]
,
[
35
]
for studies of the space and time complexity of systems centered around weak reduction.
and because in philosophy and linguistics it is commonly thought that lambda abstraction over states produces intensional contexts that are not necessarily closed under entailment relations.
16
16

16

[
32
, §6.4]
,
[
40
, pp. 73ff]
,
[
19
, §2.4]
.
Using an intensional version of combinatory logic, we show in §
6.6
that:

Theorem 7

(Conservation of weak
\beta
-equalities in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
over
\beta\eta
-equalities in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
)
.

Suppose that
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
in a signature with no constants of state type.

If
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{w\beta}M=N
then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
.

Note that this result concerns weak
\beta
-equality in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, but ordinary
\beta\eta
-equality in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. It is not obvious whether this result continues to hold when one adopts just
\beta
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and whether it continues to hold when one adopts weak
\beta
-equality on both sides; likewise it is not obvious whether it holds when constants of state type are included in the signature.
17
17

17

The obstacles are in Proposition
56
(
2
) for constants of state type, and in Proposition
66
for weakness and
\eta
.

As this discussion suggests, combinatory logic is important for the study of the metatheory of the simply-typed modal lambda calculus. The basic idea of combinatory logic is that it axiomatizes core lambda terms and their behavior under beta reduction.
18
18

18

Combinatory logic was initially developed by Curry
[
25
]
,
[
26
]
. For a modern treatment, see
[
14
]
. For a side-by-side development of combinatory logic and lambda calculus, see
[
47
, Chapters 1-9]
.
Given its close relation to the lambda calculus, today combinatory logic is most used in theoretical computer science, and is for instance the paradigmatic example of a term rewriting system.
19
19

19

[
81
, p. 64]
.
I hope this use of combinatory logic helps to make more of a case for its deployment in philosophy and linguistics.
20
20

20

Combinatory logic has one prominent advocate in linguistics, namely Steedman
[
77
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
78
]
. According to Partee’s history in
[
61
, §3]
, it was also used in early still-unpublished papers of Terence Parsons (of which I have been unable to find copies). Finally, combinatory logic has a rich history in philosophy, and was the original site of Curry’s formalism
[
28
]
and his paradox
[
27
]
,
[
71
, p. 832]
,
[
74
]
.
Combinatory logic is centrally used in the proof of Theorem
5
and Theorem
7
. The latter goes through Church-Rosser for combinatory intensional logic (§
6.2
), which we establish using Takahashi’s method of complete developments.
21
21

21

[
80
]
.
By contrast, minor extensions of Friedman-Warren’s examples show that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
does not have the Church-Rosser property for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
non-maximal.
22
22

22

[
36
]
. Cf. Example
2.7
in §
2.7
.

Outside of Zimmermann, the prior work which this paper is most indebted to is that of Gallin’s book, which was his dissertation started under Montague and finished under Scott.
23
23

23

[
39
]
.
Chapters 1-2 of Gallin’s book were concerned with Montague’s original theory, in the setting of Church’s simple theory of types. This present paper can then be thought of as the pursuit of Gallin’s project where Church’s simple theory of types is replaced with the simply-typed lambda calculus and where more options are given for the number of state types and the variables allotted to them.

As this dependence on Montague makes clear, a primary reason to be interested in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is that one takes its finite number of state variables, and its restricted means of referring to states, to be accurate of some parts of the languages which we use.
24
24

24

I.e. Bach and Partee write: “Suppose you had a language that was just like a predicate calculus except that the number of distinct variables was fixed at some small number. Suppose further that most of the time the elements acting as binders of the variables (quantifiers, abstraction operators, and the like) were either invisible in the language or not syntactically distinct from the singular terms and variables themselves. What you would have would be something like English” (
[
6
]
,
[
5
, 122]
).
From this perspective, a chief import of this paper is that semantically one can freely use the more familiar system
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
in studying the ostensibly more limited system
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Further, this is compatible with adopting a more instrumentalist attitude towards
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, and this attitude would be comfortable for one who thought that modal discourse was primarily wrought with statements of possibility and contingency of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
rather than with more elaborate modes of references to states available in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. Finally, from the Montagovian perspective, the system
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is interesting because it makes available the interpretation of terms of type
A\rightarrow B
as procedures which take one from items of type
A
to items of type
B
, in keeping with the traditional interpretation of the simply-typed lambda calculus as a way of representing algorithms which meet specifications.
25
25

25

This thus gives a new perspective on the old idea of intensions as procedures, due to Dummett
[
33
, 93, cf. pp. 96, 102, 179 ff]
and Tichý
[
82
]
. But there may be more to procedures that one would want to express than can be expressed in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. See in particular the discussion of comonads at the close of §
3.4
.

Of course, formal systems do not wear their interpretations on their sleeves. While
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
was primarily designed to be a system which integrates modal logic with the simply-typed lambda calculus, it can also be used to describe a system which integrates finite-variable logics with the simply-typed lambda calculus. Finite-variable logics have been extensively studied in the context of first-order predicate logic due to their coupling of modest expressive power with desirable computational properties, at least when compared with full first-order predicate logic.
26
26

26

[
67
]
,
[
68
]
.
On the interpretation of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
on which it is a type-theoretic generalization of finite-variable logics rather than of modal logics, the terms of state types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
refer not to worlds but rather to individuals.
27
27

27

See Example
2.1
and Example
2.3
.
However, in contrast to the setting of first-order predicate logic, the main theorems of this paper point to an expressive parity between the finite-variable systems and their infinite-variable counterparts, as far as their usual semantics goes.

As a last word of introduction, let me say something to the reader coming from modal logic. One may be disappointed in the following pages by the lack of accessibility relations, the lack of options for Barcan, the lack of bisimulations, and the apparent lack of necessity operators. As for accessibility relations
R
, they can be added since one can type them as
R\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow A\rightarrow B
, where
A
is state type and
B
is a type for truth-values. The Montagovian tradition put them in the metatheory,
28
28

28

E.g.
[
32
, p. 158]
.
since they restricted to a single variable for each state type and hence
Ruv
would be ill-formed; but part of the goal in this paper is to extend Montague’s system to settings with more variables for the state types. As for Barcan, this is just for lack of space, and another sequel to this paper focuses on simply-typed variable-domain modal lambda calculus. As regards bisimulations, these are a device for showing inexpressibility, and the resolution of Zimmermann’s question (including Zimmermann’s own resolution in the standard semantics) shows that simply-typed modal lambda calculus is maximally expressive.
29
29

29

Cf.
[
88
, fn 40 p. 34]
.
Finally, regarding necessity operators: these are given by the assertion that “
\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{P}
is the function which always outputs true,” where
A
is a state type and
P\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
has a type
B
reserved for truth-values.
30
30

30

Cf.
[
39
, p. 16]
.
In general, the lack of bound state variables in
\Box P
is simulated in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
by making the set of state variables very small; this simulation is not new and is of course just the idea behind the standard translation of modal propositional logic into non-modal first-order predicate logic.

This paper is organized as follows. In §
2
the system
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is formally defined, including its types (§
2.1
), terms (§
2.2
), its relation to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
(§
2.3
), the conventions on reduction notions (§
2.4
), its alpha conversion notion defined in terms of permutations (§
2.5
), its distinctive distanced beta version (§
2.6
), and it finally it is noted that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
has strong normalization but does not have Church-Rosser (§
2.7
). In §
3
, the Henkin semantics is described (§
3.1
), it is shown that the Soundness Theorem
17
holds for these semantics and
\beta\eta
-equality (§
3.2
), it is shown that the corresponding Completeness Theorem
20
holds for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
since it has an open term model but it is noted in Example
3.3
that the minimal system of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
does not have an open term model (§
3.3
), and the section closes by showing how to use automorphisms to establish some fundamental inexpressibility results for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
(§
3.4
). In §
4
combinatory terms in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
are introduced (§
4.1
), the expanded
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
are defined and studied (§
4.2
), and Theorem
5
is proven (§
4.3
), from which Theorems
1
,
2
are established (§
4.4
), and finally Theorem
6
is proven (§
4.5
). In §
5
, Theorems
3
,
4
are proven (§
5.1
), and open terms models for non-minimal systems equipped with the
\infty
-rule are shown to exist (§
5.2
). In §
6
a pure intensional version of combinatory logic is developed and it is used to prove Theorem
7
.

Finally, before beginning, we note for ease of reference that the numbered open questions are Question
2.6
, Question
4.2
, Question
4.5
, and Question
4.5
.

2.
The systems of simply-typed modal lambda calculi

The systems of simply-typed modal lambda calculi are formed by altering the usual simply-typed lambda calculus in two ways: by a restriction on type formation and by a restriction on the number of variables. We begin with types.

2.1.
Types

{defi}

[Types]
The
atomic types
are made up of two disjoint sets, the
state types
, which may be empty, and the
basic entity types
, which must be non-empty.

The
regular types
are defined as follows:

(1)

Each basic entity type
A
is a regular type.

(2)

If
A
is a regular type and
B
is a regular type, then
(A\rightarrow B)
is a regular type.

(3)

If
A
is a state type and
B
is a regular type, then
(A\rightarrow B)
is regular type.

A
type
is a state type or a regular type.

We associate arrows to the right in the usual way, so that
A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C
is
A\rightarrow(B\rightarrow C)
. And we drop outermost parentheses.

Any choice of state types and basic entity types gives a choice of atomic types. A traditional choice is the following:

{exa}

[Montagovian atomics]
Montague took as his atomics a single state type
S
for worlds, and two basic entity types
E,T
, where
E
is for individuals and
T
is for truth-values. In the Montagovian tradition,
S\rightarrow T
is the type of propositions, and
S\rightarrow E
is the type of intensions of individuals.
31
31

31

In semantics in linguistics, one writes the type
S\rightarrow T
as
st
, and the type
S\rightarrow E
as
se
.
But
T\rightarrow S
and
E\rightarrow S
are not types since
S
is a state type. Montague worked in Church’s simple theory of types, where the truth-values in
T
are made to be just
0
and
1
. In weaker systems, one might also take
T
to be a non-atomic type
A\rightarrow A\rightarrow A
where
A
is a regular type, which is a common way of representing Booleans (e.g.
[
8
, p. 39]
). In temporal extensions of Montague’s system, one would add another state type for times. In two-dimensional extensions of Montague’s system, one would add another state type for epistemically possible worlds.

For another example, consider:

{exa}
[Higher-order logic]
In contrast to the previous example, suppose that the atomic types are the type
E
, reserved for individuals, but which is now taken to be of
state type
, and the type
T
, reserved for truth-values, which is still taken to be a basic entity type. Then the types are in one-one correspondence with the types of relational type theory, which is also known as higher-order logic.
32
32

32

[
58
, 73]
,
[
39
, 68]
. For a notional variant of Definition
2.1
specialized to Example
2.1
, consider: “Our type system will be […] defined to be the smallest which includes the letter ‘
E
’ (the ‘type of individuals’) and ‘
T
’ (the ‘type of propositions’), and is such that whenever
A
and
B
are in it and
B
is distinct from
E
, [then]
A\rightarrow B
(the type of operations that makes type-
B
things out of type-
A
things) is in it” (
[
37
, 112]
; choice of variables changed to match ours). The type
T
is described as propositions rather than truth-values because higher-order metaphysics often models it as a Boolean algebra; this is so that the modal operators can then be viewed as operators on this algebra.

For example, the type of binary relations between individuals is given by the type
E\rightarrow E\rightarrow T
. And the type of binary relations between unary relations and individuals is given by
(E\rightarrow T)\rightarrow E\rightarrow T
.
33
33

33

That is, we implicitly “undo” the currying and think of the type
(E\rightarrow T)\rightarrow E\rightarrow T
as the type
((E\rightarrow T)\times E)\rightarrow T
.

2.2.
Terms

The restrictions on variables are enforced by the following:

{defi}

[Parameter, which controls variables of state types]
The parameter
\theta
is a function which sends each state type to an element of
\{1,2,\ldots,\omega\}
(where, recall
\omega
is the least infinite cardinal).

We extend to
\theta
to all types by setting
\theta(A)=\omega
for all regular types
A
.

We define
the set of variables of type
A
to be
\{v_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA:0\leq i<\theta({A})\}
.

Of course, we quickly move to writing
u,v,w,\ldots
etc. instead of the more formal
v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\ldots
etc. But when doing so we must be careful not to exceed the number set by the parameter.

There is a natural partial order on parameters given by
\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}
iff for all state types
A
one has
\theta(A)\leq\theta^{\prime}(A)
. Hence, if
\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}
, then all of the variables of
\theta
are also variables of
\theta^{\prime}
.

Relative to these restrictions on types and variables, we define the terms in the usual way, where we assume that a collection of typed constants, called a
signature
, has been specified in advance:

{defi}
[Terms]
Let
\theta
be a parameter and let
\mathcal{D}
be a signature. Then the terms
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is signature
\mathcal{D}
are defined as follows:

(1)

Variables
: the variables
v_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
for
i<\theta(A)
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

(2)

Constants
: the constants
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
from
\mathcal{D}
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

(3)

Applications
: if
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
and
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
then the application
(MN)\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

(4)

Lambda abstracts
: if
C
is a regular type and
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and further
0\leq i<\theta(A)
, then the lambda abstract
(\lambda{v_{i}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L})\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\;A\rightarrow C
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

Formally, the signature
\mathcal{D}
ought to be displayed in the definition of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
since terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
depend on the signature
\mathcal{D}
. But in this paper we are not switching often between different signatures, and so we omit it.

We write
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\kappa}
for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
where
\theta(A)=\kappa
for all state types
A
. Montague and Zimmermann studied
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
.
34
34

34

The system
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
is Zimmermann’s IL
∗
from
[
87
, p. 67]
when the constants are restricted to be of type
A\rightarrow B
, where
A
is a state type. See
[
87
, §4.1 p. 75]
,
[
88
, pp. 17-19]
for discussion of the relation of this to Montague’s choice of object-language. As Zimmermann says (
[
87
, p. 67]
,
[
88
, p. 19]
), the idea is to find in type theory the image of Montague’s system under the standard translation (he calls it Gallin’s translation after
[
39
, pp. 61 ff]
).
The maximal system is
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, which has countably many variables for each state type.

For application, we associate to the left in the usual way, so that
PQR
is
(PQ)R
. And we drop outermost parentheses.

For nested lambda abstraction, we use vector notation and abbreviate the term
\lambda{v_{1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\cdots\lambda{v_{n}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A_{n}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}}
by
\lambda{\vec{v}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{A}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
; and we refer to
n
as the
length
of
\vec{v}
. Further, if
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
then we abbreviate the type
A_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{n}\rightarrow C
of the nested lambda abstract
\lambda{\vec{v}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{A}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
as
\vec{A}\rightarrow C
.
35
35

35

Occasionally, when
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
are all identical, we write
\lambda{\vec{v}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
instead of
\lambda{\vec{v}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{A}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
; and we refer to the type of this term as
A^{n}\rightarrow C
where
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
. Since we use this notation sparingly, we remind the reader when we use it.
We similarly use vector notation in writing
\vec{N}
for
N_{1}\cdots N_{n}
when these are of the appropriate type; and we similarly write
\big(\lambda{\vec{v}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{A}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)\vec{N}
for
\big(\lambda{v_{1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\cdots\lambda{v_{n}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A_{n}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}}\big)N_{1}\cdots N_{n}
when
N_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{1},\ldots,N_{n}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{n}
.

Officially, everything is Church-typed rather than Curry-typed, but we minimize the display of types on the terms to maintain readability. However, we always display the type on the bound variables since the restrictions on the number of variables is so central to the system.

Here is a simple but important proposition:

Proposition 8

(Terms of state type)
.

The only terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
of state type are the variables and constants.

Proof 2.1
.

Suppose
B
is a state type. A term of type
B
cannot be an application
MN
since then we would have
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
and
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, but
A\rightarrow B
is not a type since
B
is a state type. Also, a term of type
B
cannot be a lambda abstract since lambda abstracts always have functional type and
B
is a state type and so atomic. Hence, the only remaining options for terms are constants and variables.

This proposition would fail if one tried to restrict the variables without introducing restrictions on types, since e.g. one could introduce terms of state type by mapping from a functional type into the state type.

To illustrate the usefulness of constants, consider:

{exa}
[Actuality operators: named worlds vs. diagonals]
The simplest version of the actuality operator is
\lambda{p}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{pc}
, which has type
(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B
, where
A
is a state type,
B
is a type reserved for truth-values, and
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a constant. If
c
is the actual world, then the actuality operator just takes a proposition
p
and evaluates it at the actual world.

There is also a distinct actuality operator
\lambda{p}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow A\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{pvv}}
. If
B
is again a type reserved for truth-values, then this term intuitively takes the proposition
p
, which takes two state arguments, and when given a single state argument
v
returns the diagonal
pvv
. As a lambda term, this actuality operator is the Warbler of combinatory logic (Definition
4.1
, cf. Definition
6.1
for pure combinatory logic). In modal logic itself, this actuality operator is widespread in two-dimensional logics (cf.
[
30
]
,
[
57
]
).

2.3.
Non-modal simply-typed lambda calculus

We introduce some notation, prefigured in §
1
, for the ordinary non-modal simply-typed lambda calculus:

{defi}
[Ordinary simply-typed lambda calculus]
The ordinary simply-typed lambda calculus
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is the simply-typed modal lambda calculus
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
without any state types.

If there are no state types, then the definition of type and term (Definitions
2.1
,
2.2
) just results in the ordinary simply-typed lambda calculus (
[
8
, Part I]
,
[
47
, Chapters 10, 12]
). As usual, there are as many systems of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
as there are choices of atomic basic entity types. In the proofs of Theorems
3
-
4
in §
5
, we adopt the following convention:

{defi}
[Convention on a pair of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
]
When discussing the relation between a specific pair
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
(e.g. as regards conservativity or expressibility), we assume that the basic entity types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
are the union of the state types and basic entity types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
.

We do not need this convention until §
5
, and remind the reader of it there. But we illustrate with two examples.

{exa}

[Montagovian atomics revisited]
Recall from Example
2.1
that Montague’s atomics were state type
S
, for worlds, and basic entity types
E,T
, for individuals and truth-values, respectively. While Montague himself worked in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
, we could consider working in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
as well.

Following the convention in Definition
2.3
, the associated
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has no state types but has basic entity types
S,E,T
. It is just the ordinary simply-typed lambda calculus with the three atomic types
S,E,T
.

For instance,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has types
T\rightarrow S
and
E\rightarrow S
, but
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
does not.

Further,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has terms of type
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
which are not terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, such as
Uv
, where
U\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptE\rightarrow S
and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptE
are variables.

{exa}

[Higher-order logic revisited]
Recall from Example
2.1
that higher-order logic (also known as relational type theory) has state type
E
for individuals and basic entity type
T
for truth-values (or propositions).

Relative to this choice of atomics, the systems
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with
\theta(E)<\omega
are higher-order versions of the extensively studied finite-variable first-order logics.
36
36

36

[
67
]
,
[
68
]
.
But in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
the finite variable restrictions are imposed only on the state types, which in this example are reserved for the individuals. And
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
is just a notational variant of higher-order logic.

Following the convention in Definition
2.3
, the associated
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has no state types but has basic entity types
E,T
. It is just the ordinary simply-typed lambda calculus with the two atomic types
E,T
. That is, in this example,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is the functional type theory associated to a traditional relational type theory
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
.

For instance,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has types
T\rightarrow E
and
E\rightarrow E
, but
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
does not.

Further,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has terms of type
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
which are not terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, such as
Uv
, where
U\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptE\rightarrow E
and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptE
are variables.

2.4.
Conventions on reduction notions

For a family of binary relations
R_{A}
of terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
of type
A
, we define
\rightarrow_{R_{A}}^{\theta}
to be its compatible closure, i.e. the smallest binary relation on terms of type
A
which includes
R_{A}
which is
compatible
:

(i)

It is closed under well-formed application on both sides.

(ii)

It is closed under lambda abstraction.

Regarding (
i
), this means: if
P\rightarrow_{R_{A}}^{\theta}Q
then
MP\rightarrow_{R_{B}}^{\theta}MQ
for all terms
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
; and likewise if
M\rightarrow_{R_{A\rightarrow B}}^{\theta}N
then
MP\rightarrow_{R_{B}}^{\theta}NP
for all terms
P\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Note that “closed under well-formed application on both sides” does
not
mean: if
P\rightarrow_{R_{A}}^{\theta}Q
and
M\rightarrow_{R_{A\rightarrow B}}^{\theta}N
, then
MP\rightarrow_{R_{A\rightarrow B}}^{\theta}NQ
. This would be a parallel reduction notion (cf. §
6.2
), whereas the intended idea is a single
R
-reduction happening somewhere inside the term.

Regarding (
ii
), this means: if
P\rightarrow_{R_{A}}^{\theta}Q
then
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{P}\rightarrow_{R_{A}}^{\theta}\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Q}
for every variable
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.
37
37

37

In the case of equalities, this closure condition is traditionally known as the
\xi
-rule; in the case of reductions, it usually is not given a specific name (cf.
[
9
, pp. 23, 50]
,
[
41
, pp. 13, 30]
).

We define the family
\twoheadrightarrow_{R_{A}}^{\theta}
as the reflexive transitive closure of the family
\rightarrow_{R_{A}}^{\theta}
, and we define the family
=_{R_{A}}^{\theta}
as the smallest equivalence relation containing
\rightarrow_{R_{A}}^{\theta}
. In all this, we are just following the standard treatment in
[
9
, p. 50]
, adapted to the typed context. By the same argument as
[
9
, p. 52 Lemma 3.1.6]
one has that both the family
\twoheadrightarrow_{R_{A}}^{\theta}
and the family
=_{R_{A}}^{\theta}
are compatible.

Similarly, for two families of binary relations
R_{A},S_{A}
of terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
of type
A
, we define
\rightarrow_{R_{A}S_{A}}^{\theta}
to be
\rightarrow_{R_{A}\cup S_{A}}^{\theta}
. And likewise for three families etc.

When the type
A
is clear from context, we just drop it from the subscripts of the definitions in the previous paragraph; since it is almost always clear from context, we almost always drop it. Further, in what follows, to slightly compress discussion, we often use
\rightarrow_{R}
to introduce a binary relation
R
directly, allowing ourselves to skip the extra step of first declaring
R
and then its compatible closure.

If
R_{A}
is any family of binary relations on terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
of type
A
, then for terms
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, we define
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{R}M=N
iff
M=_{R_{A}}^{\theta}N
. Note that there is no identity in the object language of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, and rather this is a meta-theoretically defined notion.

Finally, systems which keep (
i
) but which drop (
ii
) are known as weak systems, and we designate the associated reducibility relation with a ‘
w
’ prefix, and we write the associated reduction relation as
\rightarrow_{wR}^{\theta}
. We do not use weak systems until §
6
, and we remind the reader of the convention when we come to it.

2.5.
Alpha conversion

It is standard in lambda calculus to identify
\alpha
-equivalent terms, that is, terms which are the same up to renaming of bound variables, and to view oneself as formally working with equivalence classes of
\alpha
-equivalent terms (
[
9
, p. 26, pp. 577 ff]
,
[
47
, p. 277 ff]
). We proceed similarly with
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

But different choices of
\theta
result in different equivalence classes. For, if
\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}
, then the
\alpha
-equivalence classes of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
are finer than the
\alpha
-equivalence classes of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta^{\prime}}
. Here is an example where the equivalence classes are maximally fine:

{exa}
[Example of fineness of
\alpha
-equivalence classes]
In
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
, if
A
is a state type and
B
is a basic entity type and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
and
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
are variables, then
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{u}
is the only term in its
\alpha
-equivalence class. This is because formally
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is
v_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
and there are no other variables of state type
A
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
.

In many treatments of the lambda calculus, it is useful to present a step-by-step reduction notion which slowly rewrites a term into an
\alpha
-equivalent (
[
9
, p. 26]
,
[
47
, p. 278]
). But in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2}
, implementing this usual procedure would require moving to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
for
n>2
, since if one is in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2}
and working with state type
A
, if one needs to change
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cuv}}
into
\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cvu}}
, then one will have to appeal to other variables to do the transition inductively. Rather than set up this procedure, in the few places where we need a formal definition of
\alpha
-equivalence (cf. Proposition
60
), we define
\alpha
-equivalence in terms of permutations. While not common, this approach has been independently developed by Gabbay and Pitts.
38
38

38

[
38
]
,
[
63
]
,
[
64
, Chapter 4]
.

{defi}

[
\alpha
-equivalence]
Let parameter
\theta
be fixed.

Suppose that
\pi
is a type-preserving permutation of the variables of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Then we extend to a type-preserving permutation from terms
M
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
to terms
M^{\pi}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
by further setting
c^{\pi}
to be
c
for constants
c
; by setting
(M_{0}M)^{\pi}
to be
M_{0}^{\pi}M_{1}^{\pi}
; and by setting
(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{\pi}
to be
\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{\pi}}
, where
\pi(v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA)=u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
.

We define
\alpha_{A}
to be the binary relation on ordered pairs of terms
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
of type
A
given by: the ordered pair
(M,N)
stands in the
\alpha_{A}
relation iff
N
is
M^{\pi}
for some type-preserving permutation
\pi
of the variables of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
which is the identity on the free variables of
M
. Then, we define
=_{\alpha_{A}}^{\theta}
to be the smallest equivalence relation containing the compatible closure
\rightarrow_{\alpha_{A}}^{\theta}
of
\alpha_{A}
(cf. §
2.4
).

Finally, we say that two terms
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
are
\alpha
-equivalent
if
M=_{\alpha_{A}}^{\theta}N
.

Here are two brief illustrations:

{exa}

In
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2}
, suppose
A
is a state type and
B
is a basic entity type and
d\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
is a constant and
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B
is a constant and
x,y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are distinct variables. Using the permutation which transposes
x,y
we obtain that
\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{dy}
is
\alpha
-equivalent to
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{dx}
. Then the closure of
\alpha
-equivalence under application gives that
c(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{dx})(\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{dy})
is
\alpha
-equivalent to
c(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{dx})(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{dx})
.

{exa}

In
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2}
, suppose
A
is a state type and
B
is a basic entity type and
d\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow A\rightarrow B
is a constant and
x,y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are distinct variables. Then
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{dxy}
is the only formula in its
\alpha
-equivalence class. For if a permutation of
x,y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is the identity on the free variables of
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{dxy}
or its subterm
dxy
, then it is the identity permutation.

2.6.
Beta and eta reduction

The following definition is the most important definition in the paper, and developing this specific generalization of
\beta
-reduction proved instrumental to proving the main results of this paper. But the definition of this generalization is admittedly a bit baroque at first glance. For more motivation, we would point the reader forward towards the subsequent Remark
11
for a helpful heuristic. Likewise, we would point the reader towards §§
4.1
-
4.3
which culminates in the proof of Theorem
5
, where this generalization seems the almost inevitable way to extend Andrews’ proof of the combinatorial characterization of models to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.
39
39

39

While our notion came about in trying to extend Andrews’ proof, it bears some resemblance to Accattoli’s distant B-reduction
[
1
, §4.1 p. 13]
. This similarity merits further investigation, but one can say the following: while both allow “abstraction and the argument to interact at a distance” (
[
1
, §4.1 p. 13]
), our distanced
\beta
-reduction takes place in the simply-typed non-weak setting with no explicit substitutions, and Accattoli’s takes place in the untyped weak setting with explicit substitutions. That said, in §
6
, we too move to the weak setting.

{defi}

[Definition of
\beta
- and
\eta
-reduction in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
]
We say
\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}
if each of the following conditions holds

(1)

N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
;

(2)

the variables in
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
are not free in
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
;

(3)

the variables in
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B},v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are pairwise distinct.

The
distance
of the
\beta
-reduction is the length of vector
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
.

We use
\beta_{0}
for distance zero
\beta
-reduction.

We say that an instance of
\beta
-reduction is
regular
if

(4)

the only variable, if any, of state type in the tuple
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}\equiv x_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{n}
is the first one
x_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{1}
.

We use
\beta_{r}
for regular
\beta
-reduction.

Finally, we define
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Mx}\rightarrow_{\eta}^{\theta}M
when
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
not free in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
.

Now we turn to various notational remarks about
\beta
:

Remark 9

(Notation related to
\beta
)
.

The notion of “free for” in (
1
) is standard: a term
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is
free for

v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
L
if all free occurrences of
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
L
do not occur in a subterm
P
of a subterm
\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{P}
of
L
where
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is free in
N
.

The vector
\vec{M}
in
\beta
-reduction has type
\vec{M}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
, that is, the same type as that of the variables
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
.

Note that we are using
L[x:=N]
for substitution, instead of
[N/x]L
. We prefer
L[x:=N]
since it matches nicely with the familiar notation for variable assignments (cf. §
3.1
).

We adopt the convention of taking note of the distance in a
\beta
-reduction in the accompanying text whenever it is non-zero. This helps one to easily see where the more distinctive instances of our generalised
\beta
-reduction are being used in the proofs: in particular, one can just search the document for the word “distance.” Unless a result concerns what can be done with
\beta_{0}
reduction alone, we tend not to mark
\beta_{0}
explicitly but just refer to the instance as
\beta
; and similarly for
\beta_{r}
.

We take note of the following elementary proposition, which follows directly from the definitions.

Proposition 10
.

P\rightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}Q
implies
P\rightarrow_{\beta_{r}}^{\theta}Q
, which in turn implies
P\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}Q
.

One can render distanced
\beta
-reduction more familiar by defining a notion of transposition reduction:
40
40

40

Thanks to an anonymous referee for emphasizing this.

{defi}
[Transposition reduction
\tau
]
Suppose that variables
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B},\vec{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
are pairwise disjoint. Then one defines the
transposition reduction

\tau
by
\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{\vec{v}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{A}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}\vec{N}\rightarrow_{\tau}^{\theta}\big(\lambda{\vec{v}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{A}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{N}\vec{M}
.

Remark 11

(
\beta
-reduction as the composition of transposition and
\beta_{0}
-reduction)
.

If the conditions (
1
)-(
3
) of Definition
2.6
are satisfied, then one has that
\beta
is the composition
\beta_{0}\circ\tau
, in the sense of the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)N\vec{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{0}}
\textstyle{\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}}

(1)

Expressed in words, the idea is: to do a
\beta
-reduction, first check that the conditions (
1
)-(
3
) of Definition
2.6
are satisfied, and second transpose, and third do a
\beta_{0}
-reduction.

Taking note of this composition is certainly a useful heuristic, which no doubt leaves one better positioned to work, at the outset, with
\beta
-reduction of non-zero distance than by trying to primitively memorize conditions (
1
)-(
3
) of Definition
2.6
. For instance, on the basis of the composition, one sees quickly that conditions (
1
)-(
3
) of Definition
2.6
ensure that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}
, which permits the
\beta_{0}
-reduction in (
1
).

While the composition is a useful conceptualization of
\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}
, it would not be appropriate to work in
\rightarrow_{\beta_{0}\tau}^{\theta}
i.e. the compatible closure of the binary relation which is the union of
\beta_{0}
-reduction and
\tau
-reduction. For,
\tau
-reduction and hence
\beta_{0}\tau
-reduction is trivially not strongly normalizing since one has loops

(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}})PQ\rightarrow_{\tau}^{\theta}(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}})QP\rightarrow_{\tau}^{\theta}(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}})PQ\rightarrow_{\tau}^{\theta}\cdots

By contrast, one can show that
\beta
-reduction is strongly normalizing in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
(cf. §
2.7
).

Here is an example of a
\beta
-reduction of distance
1
:

{exa}
[
De re
vs.
de dicto
example of
\beta
-reduction]
Consider the proposition that the baliff thinks the defendant is a chef. The proposition has two traditional readings:

De re
: The proposition
u
that the baliff
u
thinks
v
that the defendant
u
is a chef
v
.

\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(bu\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cv(du)}\big)}

De dicto
: The proposition
u
that the baliff
u
thinks
v
that the defendant
v
is a chef
v
.

\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(bu\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cv(dv)}\big)}

These are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2}
of type
S\rightarrow T
, where the types
S,E,T
are as in Example
2.1
. Further, suppose that the constants have the types
b\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptS\rightarrow(S\rightarrow T)\rightarrow T,c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptS\rightarrow E\rightarrow T,d\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptS\rightarrow E
.

In the Montagovian tradition, one obtains the
de re
reading from the
de dicto
reading by replacing defendent
v
(i.e.
dv
) with a fresh variable (say
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptE
), lambda abstracting over that variable, and then applying the resulting lambda abstract to the value of the defendent
u
(i.e.
du
).
41
41

41

[
32
, pp. 206-207]
,
[
40
, p. 184]
. I am using the
de re
vs.
de dicto
example to illustrate distanced
\beta
-reduction. See
[
49
]
for a recent discussion of the empirical adequacy of the Montagovian perspective on the
de re
and
de dicto
.
This results in
\big(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{E}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{bu\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cvx}\big)}}\big)(du)
. While this term has the right type, namely
S\rightarrow T
, it has a free state variable
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptS
, whereas both the
de re
and
de dicto
reading are closed; further, it will not
\beta
-reduce to the
de re
reading since
du
is not free for
x
in
\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{bu\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cvx}\big)}
. To get the requisite generality and closed term, one should additionally apply the state variable
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptS
to get a term of type
T
and then lambda abstract over
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptS
one more time. If one does so, then one can derive the
de re
reading, where the first step is a
\beta
-reduction of distance 1 happening under the
\lambda
-abstract
\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{}\ldots
:

\displaystyle\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\bigg(\big(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{E}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{bu\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cvx}\big)}}\big)(du)u\bigg)}\rightarrow_{\beta}^{2}\;\;

\displaystyle\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\bigg(\big(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{E}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{bu\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cvx}\big)}\big)(du)\bigg)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{2}\;\;

\displaystyle\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\bigg(bu\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cv(du)}\big)\bigg)}

Note that it is
not
possible to use a
\beta
-reduction of distance zero on the first line since
du
is not free for
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptE
in
\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{bu\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{S}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cvx}\big)}
. (Finally, note again that the 2 superscript on
\rightarrow_{\beta}^{2}
indicates that we are working in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{2}
).

While this example shows how
\beta
-
reductions
of distance
>0
are different than
\beta
-reductions of distance zero, the following proposition shows that these differences are not present when we restrict to regular
\beta
-
equality
:

Proposition 12
.

Suppose that
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

If
M\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta_{r}}^{\theta}N
then
M=_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}N
. Hence:
M=_{\beta_{r}}^{\theta}N
iff
M=_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}N
.

Proof 2.2
.

Suppose that
\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N\rightarrow_{\beta_{r}}^{\theta}\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}
. We must show
\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N=_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}
. Let
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}\equiv x_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{\ell}
. By Definition
2.6
(
4
), we have that
B_{i}
is regular for
1<i\leq\ell
. Hence by
\alpha
-conversion we may assume that
x_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{i}
for
1<i\leq\ell
does not appear free in
\vec{M}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N

\displaystyle\twoheadleftarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}\bigg(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\bigg(\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{x}N}\bigg)\bigg)\vec{M}

(2)

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(({\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}})N}\big)\big)\vec{M}\rightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}

In the first step, we do as many
\beta_{0}
-reductions as the length
\ell
of
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
. This has the stated effect because Definition
2.6
(
2
) says that
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
is not free in
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Further, for each
1\leq i\leq\ell
one has that
x_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{i}
does not occur free in
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{1},\ldots,M_{i-1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{i-1}
. For
i=1
this is vacuously true, and for
1<i\leq\ell
this is by our previous
\alpha
-conversions. Hence, for each
1\leq i\leq\ell
,
M_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{i}
is free for
x_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{i}
in the term
\big(\lambda{x_{i+1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B_{i+1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\cdots\lambda{x_{\ell}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B_{\ell}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L})}}}\big)M_{1}\cdots M_{i-1}x_{i}\cdots x_{\ell}N
.

Finally, regarding the first step in (
2
), note that by
Definition
2.6
(
3
), we have the pairwise distinctness of the variables in the vector
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
. This ensures that the iterated
\beta
-reductions have the effect displayed in the first line. For, this condition prohibits
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
e.g. being
x_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{1},x_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{1}
; if we had this then the left-hand side of (
2
) would read
\big(\lambda{x_{1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{x_{1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}}\big)M_{2}M_{2}N
instead of
\big(\lambda{x_{1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{x_{1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}}\big)M_{1}M_{2}N
.

The second
\beta
-reduction in (
2
) follows from the variable
x_{i}
being free for itself, and from the pairwise distinctness of the variables in the vector
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
. The third
\beta
-reduction follows from Definition
2.6
(
1
), namely,
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
being free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
L
.

We add however that we do not know the answer to the following question:

{qu}

For
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, is it the case that
M=_{\beta}^{\theta}N
iff
M=_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}N
? What if
\eta
is added?

The previous proposition and examples concern what one can do with
\beta,\beta_{0},\beta_{r}
-reductions in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Here is a simple example which shows how the limited number of variables in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
can prevent even a
\beta_{0}
-reduction:

{exa}
[An example of when lack of variables prevents
\beta_{0}
-reduction]
Suppose
B
is a state type,
C
is a regular type, and
v_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB\rightarrow B\rightarrow C
are variables. If
0<j<\theta(B)
then one has the following
\beta_{0}
-reductions:

\displaystyle{\bigg(\lambda{V}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{(B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{j}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{V(vv_{j})}}\bigg)\big(\lambda{U}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uv_{0}}\big)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}\;\;

\displaystyle\lambda{v_{j}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(\lambda{U}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uv_{0}}\big)(vv_{j})}\rightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}\lambda{v_{j}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{vv_{j}v_{0}}

(3)

However if
\theta(B)=1
and
j=0
, then one does not have the first step of this
\beta
-reduction, since
\lambda{U}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uv_{0}}
is not free for
V\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt(B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow C
in the term
\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(V(vv_{0}))}
. Further, one cannot use
\alpha
-conversion to change
\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(V(vv_{0}))}
since
\theta(B)=1
.

For sake of future reference, we extend the following example ever so slightly by putting it under lambda abstracts. The naturalness of this example will emerge in §
4.1
when we recognize its initial term as the Cardinal
{\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}
of combinatory logic. Despite its humble appearance, it plays a leading role in the proof of Theorem
1
, proven in §
4.4
.

{exa}

[A naturally occurring example of a
\beta_{0}
-reduction under lambda abstracts]
Suppose
B
is a state type,
C
is a regular type. If
0<j<\theta(B)
then one can put the previous example under two
\lambda
-abstracts as follows:

\displaystyle\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{{\bigg(\lambda{V}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{(B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{j}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{V(vv_{j})}}\bigg)\big(\lambda{U}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uv_{0}}\big)}}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}\;\;

\displaystyle\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{j}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(\lambda{U}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uv_{0}}\big)(vv_{j})}}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}\;\;

\displaystyle\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{j}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{vv_{j}v_{0}}}}

(4)

As with the previous example, this is unavailable if
\theta(B)=1
.

Since the reduction is happening under lambda abstracts, this reduction is unavailable in the weak setting (cf. end of §
2.4
and outset of §
6
).

2.7.
Church-Rosser and Strong Normalization

Recall that
R
satisfies
Church-Rosser
if whenever
M\twoheadrightarrow_{R}N_{0}
and
M\twoheadrightarrow_{R}N_{1}
, then there is
L
such that
N_{0}\twoheadrightarrow_{R}L
and
N_{1}\twoheadrightarrow_{R}L
. Further,
\rightarrow_{R}
is
strongly normalizing
if for each
M
there is no infinite sequence
\{M_{i}:i\geq 0\}
with
M_{0}\equiv M
and
M_{i}\rightarrow_{R}M_{i+1}
for all
i\geq 0
. An
R
-
normal form
is an
N
such that there is no
L
with
N\rightarrow_{R}L
. Hence, strong normalization implies that for every
M
there is normal form
N
such that
M\twoheadrightarrow_{R}N
.

Since
\beta_{0}
and
\eta
reductions preserve
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, one has that Church-Rosser for
\beta_{0}
-reduction and
\beta_{0}\eta
-reduction in the ordinary simply typed lambda calculus
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
implies that
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{0}\eta_{0}
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
satisfy Church-Rosser.
42
42

42

[
8
, Proposition 1.1.9 p. 7]
.
However, Church-Rosser for
\beta
-reduction and
\beta\eta
-reduction fails for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with
\theta
non-maximal. This was noted for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
by Friedman and Warren,
43
43

43

[
36
, p. 323]
.
and their example generalizes.

{exa}

[Church-Rosser fails for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with
\theta
non-maximal]
We work in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
where for some state type
A
, one has that
\theta(A)=n
for some natural number
n\geq 1
. Let
B
be a regular type. Suppose that
u_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB\rightarrow A\rightarrow B,u_{2}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B,u_{3}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B
are variables. Further, suppose
u_{4}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A\rightarrow B\rightarrow B
is a variable, where there are
n-1
many
A
’s (and in the case
n=1
, there are no
A
’s and
u_{4}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB\rightarrow B
). Consider the following
\lambda
-term of type
B
:

P\equiv\bigg[\lambda{z_{1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\bigg(\big[\lambda{z_{2}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(u_{3}(\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{u_{4}v_{1}\cdots v_{n-1}z_{2}})(u_{1}z_{1})\big)}\big]}z_{1}\bigg)\bigg](u_{2}v_{0})

(5)

By contracting
P
’s outer redex (the one starting with
\lambda{z_{1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\ldots}
) we get:

P\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}Q_{1}\equiv\big[\lambda{z_{2}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(u_{3}(\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{u_{4}v_{1}\cdots v_{n-1}z_{2}})(u_{1}(u_{2}v_{0}))\big)}\big](u_{2}v_{0})

(6)

Then
Q_{1}
cannot be
\beta
-reduced, since
u_{2}v_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is not free for
z_{2}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
in the term
u_{3}(\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{u_{4}v_{1}\cdots v_{n-1}z_{2}})(u_{1}(u_{2}v_{0})
; and this term is the only term in its
\alpha
-equivalence class since we are in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and the other
n-1
many variables of type
A
appear freely under the scope of the
\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\ldots}
. Then
Q_{1}
is a
\beta
normal form in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Further,
Q_{1}
is not of the right form to be
\eta
-reduced. Hence
Q_{1}
is in
\beta\eta
normal form in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

By contacting
P
’s inner redex (the one starting with
\lambda{z_{2}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\ldots}
) we get:

P\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}Q_{2}\equiv\bigg[\lambda{z_{1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\bigg(\big(u_{3}(\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{u_{4}v_{1}\cdots v_{n-1}z_{1}})(u_{1}z_{1})\big)}\bigg)\bigg](u_{2}v_{0})

(7)

Then
Q_{2}
cannot be
\beta
-reduced, since
u_{2}v_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is not free for
z_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
in the term
