\big(u_{3}(\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{u_{4}v_{1}\cdots v_{n-1}z_{1}})(u_{1}z_{1})\big)
; and this term is the only term in its
\alpha
-equivalence class since we are in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and the other
n-1
many variables of type
A
appear freely under the scope of the
\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\ldots}
. Then
Q_{2}
is a
\beta
-normal form in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Further,
Q_{2}
is not of the right form to be
\eta
-reduced. Hence
Q_{2}
is in
\beta\eta
normal form in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

Since
\beta_{0}
and
\eta
reductions preserve
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, one further has
\beta_{0}
-reduction and
\beta_{0}\eta
-reduction in the ordinary simply typed lambda calculus
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
being strongly normalizing entails that
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{0}\eta
reduction in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
is strongly normalizing.
44
44

44

[
8
, p. 64]
. See §
2.3
for the relation between
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.
Using this one can show:

Proposition 13
.

In
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, both
\beta
and
\beta\eta
reduction satisfies strong normalization.

Before giving the proof, it is worth noting that Example
2.7
shows that
\beta
- and
\beta\eta
-normal forms need not be unique in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
for
\theta
non-maximal.

Proof 2.3
.

Suppose that one had an infinite sequence
\{P_{i}:i\geq 0\}
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
such that
P_{i}\rightarrow_{\beta\eta}^{\theta}P_{i+1}
for all
i\geq 0
. We show that this violates strong normalization in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. For the rest of the proof, we view
P_{i}
as terms in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
.

By
\alpha
-conversion if necessary, one has that the two sides of a
\beta
-reduction are such that they
\beta_{0}
-transitively reduce to a common term. For, if
\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}
, then one has that both
\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\omega}L[\vec{x}:=\vec{M},v:=N]
and
\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\omega}L[\vec{x}:=\vec{M},v:=N]
.

Applying this to
P_{0},P_{1},P_{2}
, we have terms
Q_{0},Q_{1}
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
satisfying the following in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
:

\textstyle{P_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{0}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\beta\eta}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces P_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{0}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{0}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\beta\eta}
\textstyle{P_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{0}\eta}
\textstyle{Q_{0}}
\textstyle{Q_{1}}

By Church-Rosser for
\beta_{0}\eta
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, one can extend this chart downwards one level. Continuing in this way, one produces an infinite descending
\beta_{0}\eta
-reduction sequence in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
.

3.
Soundness, and some completeness and open term models

3.1.
Semantics

As usual, the semantics is defined in terms of frames and variable assignments; and using these one can give the inductive definition of denotation.

{defi}

[Frame]
A
frame

\mathcal{M}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is a sequence of non-empty sets
\mathcal{M}(A)
for each type
A
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
such that for all types
A\rightarrow B
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
one has that
\mathcal{M}(A\rightarrow B)
is a subset of
\{F:\mathcal{M}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(B)\}
.

A frame is
standard
if
\mathcal{M}(A\rightarrow B)=\{F:\mathcal{M}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(B)\}
.

A
decorated frame
is a frame
\mathcal{M}
together with an assignment of each constant
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
in the signature to an element
c_{\mathcal{M}}
in
\mathcal{M}(C)
.

Variable assignments are defined in the usual way, but they only have to assign the variables dictated by the parameter
\theta
.

We use
\rho
for variable assignments, and we use
\rho[v:=x]
for the
v
-variant of
\rho
which assigns
v
to element
x
.

{defi}

[Model and denotation]
A
model

\mathcal{M}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is a decorated frame of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
such that for any variable assignment
\rho
and any term
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
one has that the inductively defined denotation
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}
is an element of
\mathcal{M}(A)
:

(1)

\llbracket v_{i}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\rho(v_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA)
for variables
v_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}

(2)

\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=c_{\mathcal{M}}
for constants
c
in the signature

(3)

\llbracket MN\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}
for terms
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB\rightarrow C
and
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}

(4)

\llbracket\lambda{v_{i}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\Lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{M}(A)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho[v_{i}:=x]}}
for terms
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
of regular type
C

On the right-hand side of (
4
), the expression
\Lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{M}(A)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho[v_{i}:=x]}}
means the metatheoretically defined function
F:\mathcal{M}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(B)
given by
F(x)=\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho[v_{i}:=x]}
for
x
in
\mathcal{M}(A)
. That is, we are using
\Lambda
(capital lambda) for the metatheoretically defined lambda abstraction.

A model is
standard
if the underlying frame is standard. A synonym for model is
Henkin model
or sometimes
generalized model
.

The only way in which a decorated frame can fail to be a model is if the metatheoretically defined function in (
4
) fails to be an element of
\mathcal{M}(A\rightarrow B)
, since this may be a small subset of the set of functions
\{F:\mathcal{M}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(B)\}
. Since we often have to argue by induction on complexity of term that a decorated frame is a model, we introduce the following definition: if
\mathcal{M}
is a decorated frame of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, then
the denotation of

M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA

is well-defined
in
\mathcal{M}
if for all subterms
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
one has that
\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}
as defined in (
1
)-(
4
) are in
\mathcal{M}(B)
, for all variable assignments
\rho
.

We write
\mathcal{M}\models M=N
if
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}
for all variable assignments
\rho
. We write
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\models M=N
if for all models
\mathcal{M}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
one has that
\mathcal{M}\models M=N
. (Note that there is no identity in the object language of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, and rather this is a meta-theoretically defined notion).

Here is an elementary but useful proposition, whose proof we omit:

Proposition 14

(The semantic effect of nested lambda abstracts)
.

For any model
\mathcal{M}
and variable assignment
\rho
relative to
\mathcal{M}
and vector of terms
\vec{M}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
of length
n
and vector of variables
\vec{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
of length
n
, define a sequence of variable assignments
\rho_{0},\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{n}
by
\rho_{0}=\rho
and
\rho_{i+1}=\rho_{i}[v_{i}:=\llbracket M_{i}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}]
for
0\leq i<n
. Then for all terms
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
, one has
\llbracket\big(\lambda{\vec{v}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{A}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}\big)\vec{M}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho_{n}}
.

3.2.
The validity of the reductions and soundness

In this subsection we prove that
\beta\eta
-reductions are valid on
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
-structures, in the strong form that the well-definedness of the denotation of the redex implies the well-definedness of the denotation of the contractum.

Proposition 15
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{M}
is a decorated frame of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

(1)

For all terms
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and all variables
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and all terms
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, if the denotations of
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are well-defined in
\mathcal{M}
and
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
, then the denotation of
L[v:=N]
is well-defined and for all variable assignments
\rho
one has
\llbracket L[v:=N]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho[v:=\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}]}
.

(2)

For all terms
\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
whose denotation is well-defined in
\mathcal{M}
, if
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
and if
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
is not free in
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, then the denotation of
\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}
is well-defined in
\mathcal{M}
and
\llbracket\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}
for all variable assignments
\rho
.

Proof 3.1
.

The usual inductive proof of (
1
) works for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
(cf.
[
8
, Lemma 3.1.13 p. 98]
). For (
2
), suppose that the vector
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
has length
n
and is
x_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB_{n}
. Let
\rho
be a variable assignment relative to
\mathcal{M}
. Define variable assignment
\rho_{0}=\rho
and
\rho_{i+1}=\rho_{i}[x_{i}:=\llbracket M_{i}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}]
for
0\leq i<n
. Then one has the following:

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\llbracket\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\;\vec{M}\;N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\;\vec{M}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}\;\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}

by semantics of app.

\displaystyle=\llbracket\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho_{n}}\;\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}

by Proposition
14

\displaystyle=\llbracket\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho_{n}}\;\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho_{n}}

since
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
not free in
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA

\displaystyle=\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho_{n}[v:=\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho_{n}}]}

by semantics of
\lambda
-abs

\displaystyle=\llbracket L[v:=N]\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho_{n}}

by (
1
)

\displaystyle=\llbracket\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\;\vec{M}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}

by Proposition
14

Proposition 16
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{M}
is a decorated frame of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. For all terms
M,N
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, if
N\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta\tau\eta}M
and the denotation of
N
is well-defined in
\mathcal{M}
, then the denotation of
M
is well-defined in
\mathcal{M}
, and for all variable assignments
\rho
, one has that
\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}
.

Proof 3.2
.

The base case for
\beta
is Proposition
15
(
2
); the base case for
\tau
(cf. Definition
40
) is trivial; the base case for
\eta
is standard. The inductive steps are trivial.

We will use the previous propositions in subsequent sections. For the moment, we note the following direct consequence:

Theorem 17

(Soundness Theorem for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
)
.

If
M,N
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\beta\eta\tau}M=N
then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\models M=N
.

3.3.
Some completeness via open term models

Now we turn to completeness for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, the maximal theory, which we can prove directly using traditional arguments. We discuss, as we proceed, where the traditional arguments break down for non-maximal parameters.

The following modifies the traditional construction to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
:
45
45

45

[
8
, Definition 3.2.9 p. 109]
.

{defi}

The
open term applicative structure

\mathcal{O}_{\theta}
for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
in a signature is defined so that
\mathcal{O}_{\theta}(A)
is the set of equivalence classes
[M]
of the set of terms
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
under the equivalence relation of
=_{\beta\eta}^{\theta}
. The application operation is given pointwise
[M][N]=[MN]
. The interpretation of constants
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is given by
c_{\mathcal{O}_{\theta}}=[c]
.

In the case of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, we refer to the open term applicative structure as
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}
, and in the case of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
, we refer to the open term applicative structure as
\mathcal{O}_{n}
.

In this subsection, for the sake of simplicity we discuss
\mathcal{O}_{n}
for
n\geq 1
, rather than treating separately the cases of
\mathcal{O}_{\theta}
for general parameters
\theta
.

In the case of
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}
, we can turn the open term applicative structure into a frame in the sense of Definition
3.1
by noting that the elements
[M]
of
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}(A\rightarrow B)
are in one-one correspondence with a subclass of functions
F:\mathcal{O}_{\omega}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\omega}(B)
:
46
46

46

This is the argument of
[
8
, Proposition 3.2.10(i)]
adapted to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
.

Proposition 18
.

Every element
[M]
of
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}(A\rightarrow B)
determines a function in
\{F:\mathcal{O}_{\omega}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\omega}(B)\}
by setting
F_{[M]}[N]=[MN]
. Moreover, the map
[M]\mapsto F_{[M]}
is injective. Hence the open term applicative structure
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}
determines a frame.

Proof 3.3
.

The function is well-defined since if
M,M^{\prime}
and
N,N^{\prime}
are
\beta\eta
-equivalent and of the appropriate type, then so are
MN,M^{\prime}N^{\prime}
. To see that the function is injective, suppose
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
with
[M][L]=[N][L]
for all terms
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. Then we can choose a variable
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
which does not occur freely in
M,N
, and from
Mx=_{\beta\eta}Nx
we can infer that
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Mx}=_{\beta\eta}\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Nx}
, and then by
\eta
to
M=_{\beta\eta}N
and then to
[M]=[N]
.

However, the previous proposition is not true for
\mathcal{O}_{1}
, and hence the open term applicative structure
\mathcal{O}_{1}
does not determine a frame:

{exa}

In
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
suppose that
A
is a state type and
B
a regular type and
U\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow A\rightarrow B
is a variable. Let
M
be
\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uv_{0}v_{0}}
and let
N
be
Uv_{0}
.
47
47

47

Note that
M
is just the result of applying the Warbler to variable
U
(cf. Definition
4.1
).
Then
Mv_{0}=_{\beta\eta}^{1}Uv_{0}v_{0}
, while
Nv_{0}
is just
Uv_{0}v_{0}
. Hence
Mv_{0}=_{\beta\eta}^{1}Nv_{0}
but it is not the case that
M=_{\beta\eta}^{1}N
, as one can see by considering standard models.

It is interesting to ask why one cannot easily produce similar examples for
n>1
. The results in §
5.2
are partially explanatory of this.

For
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
one can show that the frame determined by
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}
is a model, using the traditional argument.
48
48

48

[
8
, Proposition 3.2.10 p. 110(ii)]
.

Proposition 19
.

The frame determined by the open term applicative structure
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}
is a model.

Proof 3.4
.

We show that by induction on a term
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
with free variables from the vector
\vec{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
of length
\ell
that if
\rho
is a variable assignment with
\rho(u_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{i})=[P_{i}]
for each
i<\ell
and if
P_{i}
is free for
u_{i}
in
M
for each
i<\ell
, then
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=[M[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]]
.

Suppose
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a variable
u_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{1}
. Then
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=\rho(u_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{1})=[P_{1}]=[M[u_{1}:=P_{1}]]
.

Suppose
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a constant
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
. Then
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=c_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega}}=[c]=[M]
.

Suppose it holds for
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
and
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Then one has
\llbracket MN\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=[M[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]][N[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]]
, which is equal to
[(MN)[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]]
.

Suppose it holds for
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
. We show it holds for
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
. Since
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
has free variables
\vec{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
, these are distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. We want to show that
\llbracket\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=[(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]]
. Since
\vec{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
is distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, we have that
(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]
is
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]}
, and hence we want to show that
\llbracket\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=[\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]]
. Since
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}
satisfies extensionality, it suffices to work with elements. Hence, suppose that
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. We must show the identity
\llbracket\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}[Q]=[\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]}][Q]
.

Since we are working in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, by working with an
\alpha
-equivalent of
M[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]
if need be, we may assume that
Q
is free for
v
in
M[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]
. Since
\vec{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
is distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, we have that
Q
is free for
v
in
M
as well.

Again, we must show that
\llbracket\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}[Q]=[\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]}][Q]
. By the semantics for lambda abstracts we can rewrite the left-hand side as
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho[v:=[Q]]}
, and by
Q
being free for
v
in
M[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}]
and
\beta
-conversion
we can rewrite the right-hand side as
[M[\vec{u}:=\vec{P}][v:=Q]]
. Since
P_{i}
is free for
u_{i}
in
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
, we have that
v
is not free in
P_{i}
, for each
i<\ell
. By this and the fact that
\vec{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
is distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, we can further simplify the right-hand side so that the identity reads
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho[v:=[Q]]}=[M[\vec{u}:=\vec{P},v:=Q]]
. But this identity then follows from the induction hypothesis for
M
and
\rho[v:=[Q]]
.

Note that the proof of Proposition
19
breaks down for non-maximal parameters in the second-to-last paragraph.

In the proof of Proposition
19
, we have included in the induction hypothesis the qualification “
P_{i}
is free for
u_{i}
in
M
for each
i<\ell
.” This is often not included in the proof for the ordinary simply typed lambda calculus because there one can assume a convention to the effect that
\alpha
-conversion has been applied to avoid variable capture.
49
49

49

See the appeal to the variable convention in the proof of
[
8
, Proposition 3.2.10(ii) p. 110]
.
Since we cannot institute this convention for non-maximal parameters, out of consistency we have included the qualification for the maximal parameter. The below example shows what can happen if one fails to include the qualification in a setting, such as that of this paper, where the aforementioned convention is not in force:

{exa}

Suppose that
A,B
are types with
B
regular. Suppose that
x,y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are distinct variables, and that
U\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow A\rightarrow B
is a variable. Let
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
be
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uxy}
. Let
\rho
be a variable assignment relative to
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}
with
\rho(y)=[x]
and
\rho(U)=[U]
. Then
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=\Lambda\,z:\mathcal{O}_{\omega}(A).\,[U]z[x]
. This has the same input-output behavior as
[\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uyx}]
and hence they are identical. But
[M[y:=x]]
is
[\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{U}xx]
, which is not identical to
[\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uyx}]
.

Finally, for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, we can directly show using the traditional argument:
50
50

50

[
8
, Theorem 3.2.12]
.

Theorem 20

(Completeness Theorem for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
)
.

For terms
M,N
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
iff
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\models M=N
.

Proof 3.5
.

Given Soundness (Theorem
17
), only the backwards direction needs argument. Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\models M=N
. Since the frame determined by
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}
is a model, one has that
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}
for all variable assignments
\rho
. Enumerate the free variables of
M,N
in a vector
\vec{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
of length
\ell
. Let
\rho
be the variable assignment which assigns
\rho(u_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{i})=[u_{i}]
for each
i<\ell
. Then by the result proven in Proposition
19
one has
[M]=[M[\vec{u}:=\vec{u}]]=\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{\omega},\rho}=[N[\vec{u}:=\vec{u}]]=[N]
. Then
M,N
are
\beta\eta
-equivalent.

3.4.
Automorphisms and inexpressibility

The main theorems of this paper concern the expressive power of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}

vis-à-vis

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, and in turn of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}

vis-à-vis

\boldsymbol{\lambda}
(cf. §
2.3
for the relation between
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
). However, there are also some evident inexpressibility results pertaining of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
which are simple and important to state. As in many other areas of logic, a basic tool to show inexpressibility is automorphisms. After defining them for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and noting that automorphisms preserve denotations, we show that the actuality operator from Example
2.2
, which uses constants, cannot be expressed without constants. Then we show that the comultiplication operators from some elementary comonads have type in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and are expressible in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with constants of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
but are not expressible in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, which does not have these constants.

Given a frame
\mathcal{M}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, an
internal permutation

\pi
of
\mathcal{M}
is given by a family of permutations
\pi_{A}:\mathcal{M}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(A)
of the atomic types
A
such that (i) if
A
is a basic entity type then
\pi_{A}
is in
\mathcal{M}(A\rightarrow A)
, and such that (ii) if
A\rightarrow B
is a regular type, then the permutation
\pi_{A\rightarrow B}:\mathcal{M}((A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(A\rightarrow B))
defined by
\pi_{A\rightarrow B}(f)=\pi_{B}\circ f\circ\pi_{A}^{-1}
is an element of
\mathcal{M}((A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(A\rightarrow B))
. If
\mathcal{M}
is in addition a decorated frame, then an
internal automorphism

\pi
of
\mathcal{M}
is an internal permutation such that
\pi_{C}(c_{\mathcal{M}})=c_{\mathcal{M}}
for all constants
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
of the signature. Even when
\mathcal{M}
is a model, the requirement of membership of
\pi_{A\rightarrow B}
in
\mathcal{M}((A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(A\rightarrow B))
is non-trivial when
A
is a state type since
\pi_{A}
is not an element of any
\mathcal{M}(C)
for any type
C
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, due to
A\rightarrow A
not being a type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

The following proposition is the version of the familiar “automorphisms preserve truth-values” result which is available in this setting; its proof is standard and so we omit it:

Proposition 21
.

If
\pi
is an internal automorphism of a model
\mathcal{M}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, then for all types
A
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and all terms
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
and all variable assignments
\rho
relative to
\mathcal{M}
, one has that
\pi_{A}(\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho})=\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\pi\circ\rho}
.

In this, the variable assignment
\pi\circ\rho
is defined by
(\pi\circ\rho)(v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA)=\pi_{A}(\rho(v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA))
.

As a simple application, recall from Example
2.2
the actuality operator
\lambda{f}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{fc}
where
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a constant of state type
A
. The traditional discussion in propositional and predicate modal logics is whether this actuality operator is an expressive enrichment (cf.
[
44
]
,
[
43
]
). The question in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is then whether constant symbols of state type are an expressive enrichment. The following simple application of Proposition
21
shows that they are:

Proposition 22

(Non-expressibility of actuality operator in the empty signature)
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{M}
is a standard model in the empty signature. Suppose
A
is a state type such that
\mathcal{M}(A)
has at least two elements. Suppose that
B
is a regular type formed from basic entity types and arrow such that
\mathcal{M}(B)
has at least two elements.

For each
w
in
\mathcal{M}(A)
let
F_{w}
in
\mathcal{M}((A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B)
be defined by
F_{w}(f)=f(w)
. Then for each
w
in
\mathcal{M}(A)
there is no closed expression
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
in the empty signature such that
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}=F_{w}
.

Proof 3.6
.

To see this, suppose for reductio that
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}=F_{w}
. Since
\mathcal{M}(A)
has at least two elements, choose
v
in
\mathcal{M}(A)
which is distinct from
w
. Let
\pi_{A}
be a permutation which transposes
w,v
. Since
\mathcal{M}
is standard, extend
\pi
to an internal automorphism of
\mathcal{M}
by setting
\pi_{C}
to be the identity for any basic entity type
C
and by setting, for any functional type
C\rightarrow D
and any
f
in
\mathcal{M}(C\rightarrow D)
, the identity
\pi_{C\rightarrow D}(f)=\pi_{D}\circ f\circ\pi_{C}^{-1}
. Note two things:

(1)

For any functional type
C\rightarrow D
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and any
g
in
\mathcal{M}(C\rightarrow D)
one has
\pi_{C\rightarrow D}^{-1}(g)=\pi_{D}^{-1}\circ g\circ\pi_{C}
.

(2)

For a regular type
C
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
formed from basic entity types and arrow, one has
\pi_{C}
is the identity.

For any
G
in
\mathcal{M}((A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B)
we have that
\pi_{(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B}G=\pi_{B}\circ G\circ\pi_{A\rightarrow B}^{-1}=G\circ\pi_{A\rightarrow B}^{-1}
. Further, for any
f
in
\mathcal{M}(A\rightarrow B)
one has that

(\pi_{(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B}G)f=G(\pi_{A\rightarrow B}^{-1}(f))=G(\pi_{B}^{-1}\circ f\circ\pi_{A})=G(f\circ\pi_{A})

(8)

By the reductio hypothesis and Proposition
21
one has that

\pi_{(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B}\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}=\pi_{(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B}\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\pi\circ\rho}=\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}=F_{w}

(9)

where the first and second-to-last identities follow since
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B
is closed. Then for all
f
in
\mathcal{M}(A\rightarrow B)
one has
f(w)=F_{w}(f)=(\pi_{(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow B}\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}})f=\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}(f\circ\pi_{A})=F_{w}(f\circ\pi_{A})=(f\circ\pi_{A})(w)=f(\pi_{A}(w))=f(v)
.

But since
\mathcal{M}
is standard and
\mathcal{M}(B)
has at least two elements, we can choose
f:A\rightarrow B
such that
f(w)\neq f(v)
, a contradiction.

The previous example shows that the ability of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
to have constants of state type is an expressive enrichment. But a state type
A
, viewed not as a type but as a first-order structure, may have natural options not only for constant symbols
d\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
but also for constant symbols
c:A^{k}\rightarrow A
for
k\geq 1
,
51
51

51

We use the notation from footnote
35
.
which are naturally interpreted as functions from the
k
-th Cartesian power of
A
to
A
. But of course, if
A
is a state type then
A^{k}\rightarrow A
is a type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
but not of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
(cf. §
2.3
for the relation between
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
). While
c:A^{k}\rightarrow A
may not be a constant of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, it can be used to form natural terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
which have type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, and the question is whether these are expressible in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. The following proposition shows that they need not be. (Incidentally, this proposition shows the need for the inclusion of the qualification “…its free variables and constants are those of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
” in the statement of Theorem
2
).

Proposition 23

(Non-expressibility of the comultiplication map in the empty signature)
.

Suppose that
A
is a state type and
B
a basic entity type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Suppose that
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow A\rightarrow A
is a constant of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. Let
P\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(A\rightarrow A\rightarrow B)
be the closed term
\lambda{f}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{f(cxy)}}}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. Then there is no term
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(A\rightarrow A\rightarrow B)
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
in the empty signature such that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\models P=Q
.

Proof 3.7
.

Suppose not, and let
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(A\rightarrow A\rightarrow B)
be such a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
in the empty signature. Consider the standard model
\mathcal{N}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with
\mathcal{N}(C)
equal to the natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
for each atomic type
C
, and with
c_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
being given by the curried version of a binary function
\oplus:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
subject to the constraint that there are
w,v\geq 0
and a permutation
\pi
of
\mathbb{N}
such that
\pi^{-1}(w\oplus v)\neq\pi^{-1}(w)\oplus\pi^{-1}(v)
. By restricting to the types and constants of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, this model induces a standard model
\mathcal{M}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. By our reductio hypothesis, we have
\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{N},\rho}=\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{N},\rho}
for any variable assignment
\rho
relative to
\mathcal{N}
. Since
\mathcal{M}
agrees with
\mathcal{N}
on terms of type
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
in the empty signature, we have
\llbracket P\rrbracket_{\mathcal{N},\rho}=\llbracket Q\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho_{0}}
for any for any variable assignment
\rho
relative to
\mathcal{N}
with
\rho_{0}
denoting its restriction to
\mathcal{M}
. The proof then proceeds similarly to the previous proposition, using the aforementioned constraint on
\oplus
.

The example in Proposition
23
is not
ad hoc
, but is natural in the study of comonads. Monads and its dual comonads are important in contemporary theoretical computer science: whereas monads were deployed by Moggi to model impure effects in a purely functional language,
52
52

52

[
53
]
,
[
54
]
,
[
11
]
.
comonads have been used, since Brookes and Geva, as a ‘‘semantic model in which sensible comparisons can be made between programs with the same extensional behavior.’’
53
53

53

[
15
, 2]
.

In Brookes and Geva’s original example, the type
A
is interpreted as the natural numbers with an infinite element on top,
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
, and the constant
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow A\rightarrow A
is interpreted as minimum; then, on its intended interpretation, the term
P
from Proposition
23
is the comultiplication map which sends
f
to
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\min(x,y)}}
.
54
54

54

[
15
, 8]
.
This comultiplication map “shows how a computation may itself be computed” by breaking
(f(0),f(1),\ldots)
into its initial segments which repeat the last element.
55
55

55

[
15
, 3]
. Another natural comonad is the stream comonad (cf.
[
62
, p. 160, Example 5.3.2]
).

Just as the ability of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
to express the actuality operator should be seen as a virtue of the system, so the inability of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
to express the comultiplication maps of natural comonads should be seen as a deficit. When
A
is state type and
B
is a regular type, one might try to remedy this deficit by admitting terms like
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{f(cxy)}}
into
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
while still banning
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{cxy}}
or its
\eta
-equivalent
c
. But this revision would complicate severely the metatheory developed in this paper since there would now be more complicated terms of state type like
cxy
. Indeed, on this revision, Proposition
8
would no longer hold, which is used extensively throughout the paper, in particular in the proof of Theorem
26
of the next section, on which much else in this paper depends.

4.
Combinatory logic and conservation and expressibility

4.1.
Typed combinator terms and their reductions

The following definition provides a small list of typed combinators terms
\mathsf{X}_{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}}
terms, where its type is a function of the types
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
. For each combinator term, we give

•

the traditional choice of letter
\mathsf{X}
along with the Smullyan mnemonic (cf.
[
76
]
),

•

its defining term,

•

its type built up out of
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
,

•

an intuitive gloss, using informal descriptions of input-output behaviour of functions, as well as informal functional notation such as
(x,y)\mapsto x(y)
and
(x,i)\mapsto x_{i}
.

•

an identification of the conditions on the types
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
, and the variables of these types, required in order for this to be a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

{defi}

[Typed combinator terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
]
An
Identity Bird term

{\mathsf{I}}_{A}

of

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is a term of
of the following form and type:

\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{x}\;:\;A\rightarrow A

It is required that
A
has regular type. Intuitively
{\mathsf{I}}_{A}
is the identity function on type
A
.

A
Kestrel term

{\mathsf{K}}_{A,B}

of

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is a term of the following form and type:

\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{x}}\;:\;A\rightarrow B\rightarrow A

It is required that
A
has regular type, and that
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA,y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
are distinct variables. Intuitively given a value in
A
, Kestrel
{\mathsf{K}}_{A,B}
returns the constant function from
B
to
A
with that value.

A
Cardinal term

{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}

of

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is a term of the following form and type:

\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}}}\;:\;(A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow B\rightarrow A\rightarrow C

It is required that
C
has regular type, and that
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB,z\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are distinct variables. Intuitively Cardinal
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}
takes a function
x
of two arguments and returns the function of two arguments which permutes the two inputs. I.e. it maps function
x
to the function
(y,z)\mapsto x(z,y)
.

A
Dardinal term

{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}

of

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is a term of the following form and type:

\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzc}}\;:\;(A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow A\rightarrow C

It is required that
C
has regular type and that
B
has state type and that
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a constant.
Dardinal
is short for
decorated Cardinal
. The number of Dardinals varies with the signature, and intuitively
{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}
takes a function
x
of two arguments and returns the function of one argument which slots this value into the first spot and
c
into the second spot. I.e. it maps function
x
to the function
z\mapsto x(z,c)
.

A
Starling term

{\mathsf{S}}_{A,B,C}

of

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is a term of the following form and type:

\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C\rightarrow A\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C\rightarrow A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xz(yz)}}}\;:\;(C\rightarrow A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(C\rightarrow A)\rightarrow C\rightarrow B

It is required that
A,B
are regular types. Like Cardinal, Starling
{\mathsf{S}}_{A,B,C}
permutes some of the order of the inputs, but it also is a basic example of a combinator which duplicates an input. As for its intended behaviour, as an argument of
x,y
, it is just an “indexed” version of functional application
z\mapsto x_{z}(y_{z})
.

A
Warbler term

{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}

of

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is a term of the following form and type:

\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow A\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xyy}}\;:\;(A\rightarrow A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow A\rightarrow B

It is required that
B
is of regular type. Intuitively, Warbler
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}
takes a curried function
x
defined on
A\times A
and returns the diagonal function on
A
given by
y\mapsto x(y,y)
.

A
Bluebird term

{\mathsf{B}}_{A,B,C}

of

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is a term of the following form and type:

\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{x(yz)}}}:(B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow A\rightarrow C

It is required that
B,C
are regular types; and if
A
is regular then it is required that
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB\rightarrow C,z\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are distinct variables and that
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB\rightarrow C,y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
are distinct variables. The Bluebird
{\mathsf{B}}_{A,B,C}
returns the composition of two functions
x,y
whose domains and codomains match appropriately.

Each of these terms is closed. Further, each of these terms has pairwise distinct bound variables: this follows from the stipulated distinctness in the above definition together with the distinctness of type due to some types being functions of others. This is important to take note of because distinctness of variables is a part of
\beta
-reduction (cf. Definition
2.6
(
3
)). However, we note the following:

Remark 24

(Limited availability of Cardinal)
.

The only combinator in Definition
4.1
which can have more than one bound variable of state type is Cardinal. This happens when both
A,B
in Cardinal are of state type. When both
A,B
are identical state types, the requirement that
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB,z\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are distinct variables implies that the associated Cardinal term is simply not available in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
.

Later in §
6
, when we develop pure intensional combinatory logic, we will have to pay attention to this limited availability of Cardinal. For this purpose, we note now that in Cardinal, the stated requirement that
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB,z\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are distinct variables implies: either
A,B
are distinct types, or that
A,B
are identical types and
\theta(A)=\theta(B)>1
.

While obvious from definition, we note the following about the other combinators:

Remark 25

(Availability of all typed combinator terms besides Cardinal)
.

Besides Cardinal, all typed combinator terms are
\alpha
-equivalent to a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
. This is because, by inspection, each contains at most one bound variable of a given state type.

The following theorem and subsequent two propositions say that the combinator terms have their expected behavior in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. The proof of these results also contain many useful examples of
\beta
-reductions of distance 1 and 2. For later purposes, we take note that all of these reductions are indeed regular
\beta
-reductions (cf. Definition
2.6
(
4
)).
56
56

56

While we are not pursuing weakness until §
6
, it is worth noting that the reductions in this theorem and the subsequent two propositions are weak.

Theorem 26

(Combinatory behaviour in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
)
.

Suppose that the combinators on the below left are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. For each item, suppose that the terms
P,Q,R
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
of the appropriate type to make the below applications well-formed. Then one has the
\beta
-reductions to the terms on the below right, and indeed all of these reductions are regular:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{I}}_{A}P\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}P\hskip 19.91692pt{\mathsf{K}}_{A,B}PQ\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}P

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84526pt{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQR\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}PRQ

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}PR\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}PRc

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt{\mathsf{S}}_{A,B,C}PQR\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}PR(QR)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}PQ\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}PQQ

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathsf{B}}_{A,B,C}PQR\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}P(QR)

Proof 4.1
.

For Identity Bird
{\mathsf{I}}_{A}
, we have
(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{x})P\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}P
since the term
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
has no lambda abstracts.

Now we turn to the remaining cases. In this part of the proof:

•

We restrict attention to the case where at least one of the constituent types
A,B,C
(or just
A,B
in the case of Kestrel and Warbler) is a state type. For, if all of the constituent types are regular, then by inspection of the definitions, all of the bound variables in the combinator terms are regular and we can change them by
\alpha
-conversion to avoid variable capture.

•

We repeatedly use Proposition
8
, which says that terms of state type in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
are either constants or variables of that very state type.

•

We often ensure that the “free for” condition of
\beta
-reduction (Definition
2.6
(
1
)) is met by doing
\alpha
-conversion on the bound variables of the combinatory terms to ensure that they are disjoint from the free variables of the inputs. We refer to this simply as “disjointness.”

•

As we proceed, we note that the reductions meet the regularity condition Definition
2.6
(
4
), but do not say more than this since it follows clearly from the displayed instances and the case assumptions. Further, so as not to clutter the proof, we just mark regularity in the text and write the simpler
\beta
instead of
\beta_{r}
. Finally, since all
\beta
-reductions of distance
\leq 1
are trivially regular, we only need to explicitly take note of regularity for instances of distance
\geq 2
(as a matter of fact, all
\beta
-reductions in this proof have distance
\leq 2
).

For Kestrel
{\mathsf{K}}_{A,B}
, suppose
A
is a regular type,
B
is a state type, and
P\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
and
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. We can use
\alpha
-conversion on the bound variable of type
A
in Kestrel so that it does not appear free in
Q
. Further,
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is free for Kestrel’s second variable
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
in the term
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, since the latter has no lambda abstracts. These two points get us the following, where the first is a
\beta
-reduction of distance
1
:

\big(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{x}}\big)PQ\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{x[y:=Q]})P\equiv(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{x})P\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}P

(10)

The second
\beta
-reduction follows just as in Identity Bird.

For Cardinal
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}
, suppose that
C
is a regular type and suppose we have the terms
P\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B\rightarrow C
and
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
and
R\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Suppose the corresponding three bound variables of Cardinal
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}
are
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B\rightarrow C
and
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
and
z\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. We respectively refer to these in the following discussion as the
first
,
second
, and
third bound variables
of Cardinal (and we adopt similar conventions for the subsequent combinator terms).

There are three cases to consider.

If
A
is a regular type and
B
is a state type, then since the first and third bound variables are of regular type, we may change them by
\alpha
-conversion so that they do not appear free in
P
or
R
; and they do not appear free in
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
since
B
is of state type. Then
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is free for
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
in
\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}
. Further, the first bound variable does not appear free in
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
since
B
is of state type. This gives us the first step in the following, which is a
\beta
-reduction of distance
1
:

\displaystyle(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}}})PQR

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\;(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzQ}})PR

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\;(\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{PzQ})R

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\;PRQ

(11)

The second
\beta
-reduction follows by disjointness: the third bound variable does not appear free in
P
by the previous
\alpha
-conversion; and
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
does not contain any bound variables since
B
is of state type . The third
\beta
-reduction follows since the displayed free occurrence of
z\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is the only free occurrence in
PzQ
, since by previous
\alpha
-conversion it does not appear free in
P
, and since
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is of state type
B
.

If
A
is a state type and
B
is a regular type, then since the first and the second bound variables are of regular type, we may change them by
\alpha
-conversion so that they do not appear free in
P
or
Q
; and they do not occur free in
R\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
since
A
is of state type. Since
xzy
contains no lambda abstracts, one has that
R\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is free for
z\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
xzy
. This gives us the first step in the following, which is a regular
\beta
-reduction of distance
2
:

\displaystyle(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}}})PQR

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\;(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xRy}})PQ

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\;(\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{PRy})Q

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\;PRQ

(12)

The second
\beta
-reduction follows by disjointness: the second bound variable does not appear free in
P
by the previous
\alpha
-conversion; and
R\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does not contain any bound variables since

is of state type. The third

-reduction follows since the displayed free occurrence of
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB

, since by previous

If

are both state types, then since the first bound variable is of regular type it does not appear free in

or

) by
\alpha

. This implies that

is free for

in
\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}
. Then we have the following, where the first is a
\beta
-reduction of distance 1:

\displaystyle(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}}})PQR

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\;(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzQ}})PR

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\;(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xRQ})P

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\;PRQ

in

since the term

and

\big(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzc}}\big)PR\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xRc})P\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}PRc

The following proposition is more elementary:

Proposition 27
.

Suppose that the combinators on the below left are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. For each item, suppose that the terms
P,Q
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
of the appropriate regular type to make the below applications well-formed. Further, suppose that the
only
free variables of
P,Q
are themselves of regular type. Then one has the
\beta
reductions to the terms on the below right, and indeed these are
\beta_{0}
-reductions:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{K}}_{A,B}P\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{P}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Pzy}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Pzc}

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{A,B,C}PQ\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Pz(Qz)}

\displaystyle{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}P\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Pyy}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathsf{B}}_{A,B,C}PQ\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{P(Qz)}

Proof 4.2
.

We give the argument for Cardinal, since the argument for the other combinators is similar. For the second and third bound variables of Cardinal, if they are of regular type then we may use
\alpha
-conversion to convert them to variables which do not occur free in
P
; while if they are of state type then by hypothesis they do not occur free in
P
. Hence, after this
\alpha
-conversion, the second and third bound variables of the Cardinal term do not occur free in
P
, and so we can use disjointness to
\beta
-reduce and indeed
\beta_{0}
-reduce.

Lastly, for later (cf. Remark
41
), we need to take note of the following reduction of Cardinal to Dardinal:

Proposition 28
.

Suppose that the below displayed Cardinal is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Suppose that the below terms
P,c
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
of the appropriate type to make the applications well-formed. Then one has the following regular reduction:

{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}Pc\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P

Proof 4.3
.

We simply use a
\beta
-reduction of distance 1:

\big(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}}}\big)Pc\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzc}})P

For, one has that
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is free for
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
in
\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}
since
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is closed. And no variables appear free in a constant.

4.2.
The BCDKW-combinatorial terms

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{X}
a collection of typed combinators in a signature.

The
\mathcal{X}
-combinatorial terms of

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
in that signature are the smallest collection of terms in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
which is closed under application and which contains the constants of the signature, the variables specified by the parameter
\theta
, and all instances of combinators
\mathsf{X}_{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}}
in
\mathcal{X}
which are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

The
expanded
\mathcal{X}
-combinatorial terms of

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
in that signature is the collection of terms
N
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
such that there is a
\mathcal{X}
-combinatorial term
M
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with the same free variables as
N
satisfying
M\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}N
.

We will be mostly concerned in what follows with
\mathcal{X}
being
\{\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D},\mathsf{K},\mathsf{W}\}
, which we abbreviate as
\mathsf{BCDKW}
. In the untyped setting, one can take
\mathsf{BCKW}
or
\mathsf{SK}
as primitive (cf.
[
14
, Lemma 1.3.9 p. 17]
), and indeed historically Schönfinkel did the latter and Curry initially did the former (
[
71
, §2.2]
). We opt to use a
\mathsf{BCKW}
basis rather than a
\mathsf{SK}
basis, although see Question
4.2
and Remark
31
below.

We first note that Starling and identity are
\mathsf{BCDKW}
combinatorial.

Proposition 29

(Recovery of Starling)
.

Suppose that
A,B,C
are types and
A,B
are regular types. Then
{\mathsf{S}}_{A,B,C}
is an expanded
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

Proof 4.4
.

One can check that:

{\mathsf{B}}_{A_{1},B_{1},C_{1}}({\mathsf{B}}_{A_{2},B_{2},C_{2}}({\mathsf{B}}_{A_{3},B_{3},C_{3}}{\mathsf{W}}_{A_{4},B_{4}}){\mathsf{C}}_{A_{5},B_{5},C_{5}})({\mathsf{B}}_{A_{6},B_{6},C_{6}}{\mathsf{B}}_{A_{7},B_{7},C_{7}})\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}{\mathsf{S}}_{A,B,C}

(18)

where the types of
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i}
are calculated in terms of
A,B,C
as follows:

i

A_{i}

B_{i}

C_{i}

1

C\rightarrow A\rightarrow B

C\rightarrow(C\rightarrow A)\rightarrow C\rightarrow B

(C\rightarrow A)\rightarrow C\rightarrow B

2

C\rightarrow(C\rightarrow A)\rightarrow C\rightarrow B

(C\rightarrow A)\rightarrow C\rightarrow C\rightarrow B

(C\rightarrow A)\rightarrow C\rightarrow B

3

C\rightarrow A

C\rightarrow C\rightarrow B

C\rightarrow B

4

C

B

n/a

5

C

C\rightarrow A

C\rightarrow B

6

C

A\rightarrow B

(C\rightarrow A)\rightarrow C\rightarrow B

7

C

A

B

After checking that these types are in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and that the applications in (
18
) are well-formed, the proof of (
18
) follows the usual untyped reduction (cf.
[
25
, p. 155]
), using Theorem
26
and Proposition
27
.

Proposition 30

(Recovery of identity)
.

If
B
is a regular type then
{\mathsf{I}}_{B}
is an expanded
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. In particular, there is a
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term
N
witnessing this which satisfies
N\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}{\mathsf{I}}_{B}
.

Proof 4.5
.

One can check that
{\mathsf{S}}_{B\rightarrow B,B,B}{\mathsf{K}}_{B,B\rightarrow B}{\mathsf{K}}_{B,B}
works.

It is not obvious, from the definitions, whether the following is true:

{qu}

[Relation between
\mathsf{BCDKW}
and
\mathsf{SK}
]
Are the
\mathsf{BCDKW}
combinators expanded
\mathsf{SK}
combinatorial in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
?

However, we can show that one traditional definition of Cardinal in terms of Starling and Kestrel fails:

Remark 31

(On recovering Cardinal from Starling and Kestrel)
.

To show that
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,A,B}
is expanded
\mathsf{SK}
-combinatorial in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
for
n>1
when
A
is a state type and
B
is a regular type, one might try to follow the untyped reduction (
[
25
, pp. 158-159]
), and seek to find types
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
such that

{\mathsf{S}}_{A_{1},B_{1},C_{1}}\big({\mathsf{B}}_{A_{2},B_{2},C_{2}}\,{\mathsf{B}}_{A_{3},B_{3},C_{3}}\,{\mathsf{S}}_{A_{4},B_{4},C_{4}}\big)\big({\mathsf{K}}_{A_{5},B_{5}}{\mathsf{K}}_{A_{6},B_{6}}\big)\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{n}{\mathsf{C}}_{A,A,B}

(19)

But we will argue that this is not possible. For reductio, suppose not, and suppose that we indeed had (
19
). Then the type of the terms in (
19
) is both
C_{1}\rightarrow B_{1}
and
(A\rightarrow A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow A\rightarrow A\rightarrow B
. Hence in particular
C_{1}
is a regular type.

But then consider the following reductions:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{A_{1},B_{1},C_{1}}\big({\mathsf{B}}_{A_{2},B_{2},C_{2}}\,{\mathsf{B}}_{A_{3},B_{3},C_{3}}\,{\mathsf{S}}_{A_{4},B_{4},C_{4}}\big)\big({\mathsf{K}}_{A_{5},B_{5}}{\mathsf{K}}_{A_{6},B_{6}}\big)

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{n}\;

\displaystyle\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big({\mathsf{B}}_{A_{2},B_{2},C_{2}}\,{\mathsf{B}}_{A_{3},B_{3},C_{3}}\,{\mathsf{S}}_{A_{4},B_{4},C_{4}}\big)x\,({\mathsf{K}}_{A_{5},B_{5}}\,{\mathsf{K}}_{A_{6},B_{6}}\,x)}

by Prop.
27

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{n}\;

\displaystyle\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big({\mathsf{B}}_{A_{2},B_{2},C_{2}}\,{\mathsf{B}}_{A_{3},B_{3},C_{3}}\,{\mathsf{S}}_{A_{4},B_{4},C_{4}}\big)x\;{\mathsf{K}}_{A_{6},B_{6}}}

by Thm.
26

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{n}\;

\displaystyle\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A_{2}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{{\mathsf{B}}_{A_{3},B_{3},C_{3}}({\mathsf{S}}_{A_{4},B_{4},C_{4}}y)}\big)x\;{\mathsf{K}}_{A_{6},B_{6}}}

by Prop.
27

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{n}\;

\displaystyle\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{{\mathsf{B}}_{A_{3},B_{3},C_{3}}({\mathsf{S}}_{A_{4},B_{4},C_{4}}\,x)\;{\mathsf{K}}_{A_{6},B_{6}}}

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{n}\;

\displaystyle\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A_{3}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{{\mathsf{S}}_{A_{4},B_{4},C_{4}}\,x\,({\mathsf{K}}_{A_{6},B_{6}}}\,y)}\;

by Prop.
27

Note that
{\mathsf{K}}_{A_{6},B_{6}}\,y
being well-formed implies that
y
has type
A_{6}
, which is required to be regular (cf. Kestrel in Definition
4.1
), and together with the earlier lambda abstract
\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A_{3}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\ldots}
implies that
A_{3},A_{6}
are identical and are regular. Then we can continue:

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{n}\;

\displaystyle\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A_{3}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C_{4}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xz({\mathsf{K}}_{A_{6},B_{6}}}\,y\,z)}}

by Prop.
27

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{n}\;

\displaystyle\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C_{1}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A_{3}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C_{4}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}}}

by Thm.
26
.

If this is to be the Cardinal
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,A,B}
, then one has
C_{1}
is
A\rightarrow A\rightarrow B
and
A_{3},C_{4}
are
A
. But then
A_{3}
is a state type, contradicting our earlier conclusion that
A_{3}
is a regular type.

In the previous remark, our redutio hypothesis involved Bluebirds, and unlike Cardinal these are indeed recoverable from Starling and Kesterl:

Proposition 32

(Recovering Bluebird from Starling and Kestrel)
.

Suppose that
A,B,C
are types and
B,C
are regular types. Then
{\mathsf{B}}_{A,B,C}
is
an expanded
\mathsf{SK}
-combinatorial term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

Proof 4.6
.

Following the untyped reduction (cf.
[
25
, 158]
), one can check that:

{\mathsf{S}}_{A_{1},B_{1},C_{1}}\big({\mathsf{K}}_{A_{2},B_{2}}\,{\mathsf{S}}_{A_{3},B_{3},C_{3}}\big)\,{\mathsf{K}}_{A_{4},B_{4}}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}{\mathsf{B}}_{A,B,C}

(20)

where the types of
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i}
are calculated in terms of

as follows:

4.3.
Combinatorial characterisation of models

The following is the key result needed to establish Theorem
5
. As mentioned in §
1
, this generalizes work of Andrews for the ordinary simply-typed lambda calculus (
[
2
, Lemma 1 p. 388]
). Whereas Andrews was able to use the Starling combinator at the induction step, in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
we use Starling as well as Warbler, Cardinal, and Dardinal.
57
57

57

While we are not pursuing weakness until §
6
, we note in passing that the proof of this theorem contains many examples of non-weak
\beta
-reductions.

Theorem 33
.

Suppose
A,B
are types and
B
is a regular type and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a variable of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Then
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
is an expanded
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

Proof 4.7
.

We show by induction on complexity of the
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
with regular type
B
that for every type
A
and variable
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
there is a
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
with the same free variables as
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
such that
N\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
.

As a first case, if
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is the variable
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, then let
N
be
{\mathsf{I}}_{A}
, so that we are done by Proposition
30
.

As a second case, suppose that
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a variable
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Then
{\mathsf{K}}_{B,A}M
is
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial with the same free variables as
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
, namely
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
. Further we have
{\mathsf{K}}_{B,A}M\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
by Proposition
27
.

As a third case, suppose that
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a constant
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
. Then
{\mathsf{K}}_{B,A}M
is
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial with the same free variables as
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
, namely no free variables. Further we have
{\mathsf{K}}_{B,A}M\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
by Proposition
27
.

As a fourth case, suppose that
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is an instance of
{\mathsf{B}}_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C^{\prime}}
,
{\mathsf{C}}_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C^{\prime}}
,
{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C^{\prime}}{}
,
{\mathsf{K}}_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}
,
{\mathsf{W}}_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}
. These are closed terms, and are handled exactly as the previous case.

As a fifth case, suppose that
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
M_{0}M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
where
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC\rightarrow B
and
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
.

There are several subcases to consider.

As a first subcase, suppose that
C
is a regular type. By induction hypothesis, there are
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial terms
N_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow C\rightarrow B,N_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow C
such that
N_{0}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}
and
N_{1}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{1}}
, with the two terms
N_{i}
and
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{i}}
having the same free variables for
i=0,1
. Note that the first bound variable of
{\mathsf{S}}_{C,B,A}
has type
A\rightarrow C\rightarrow B
, which is the same type as
N_{0}
. And note that the second bound variable of
{\mathsf{S}}_{C,B,A}
has type
A\rightarrow C
, which is the type of
N_{1}
. Then
{\mathsf{S}}_{C,B,A}N_{0}N_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
is an expanded
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term by induction hypothesis and Proposition
29
; and one has
{\mathsf{S}}_{C,B,A}N_{0}N_{1}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}{\mathsf{S}}_{C,B,A}(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}})(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{1}})
. The third bound variable of
{\mathsf{S}}_{C,B,A}
is
z\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, which we can switch to
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
by
\alpha
-conversion. Further, the first two bound variables of
{\mathsf{S}}_{C,B,A}
are of regular type and so we may switch these so that they too do not appear free in
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}},\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{1}}
. Then we continue to reduce as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{C,B,A}(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}})(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{1}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\big(\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}})v(yv)}}\big)(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{1}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}})v((\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{1}})v)}

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}M_{1}}

The first
\beta
-reduction follows by disjointness: by previous
\alpha
-conversion the second bound variable
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow C
of Starling does not appear free in
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}
; and the third bound variable
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of Starling is not free in
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}
. The second
\beta
-reduction follows since the displayed free occurrence of
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow C
in
(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}})v(yv)
is its only free occurrence, since by previous
\alpha
-conversion this second bound variable does not appear free in
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}
. The two applications of
\beta
in the last line simply follow from a variable being free for itself.

As a second subcase, suppose that
C
is a state type. Since
C
is a state type, the Starling
{\mathsf{S}}_{C,B,A}
is no longer a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, and hence we need to use other combinators. By induction hypothesis, there is
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term
N_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow C\rightarrow B
such that
N_{0}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}
and such that
N_{0}
and
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}
have the same free variables. Then
N_{0}v\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}M_{0}
. By Proposition
8
the term
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a variable or a constant of state type
C
. There are three further subcases to consider.

•

First suppose that
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, which implies in particular that
A,C
are identical state types. By
\alpha
-conversion if necessary change the second bound variable of
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}
to
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. This Warbler term is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
since
B
is regular by hypothesis of the theorem. Since
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is not free in
N_{0}
we have by disjointness that

{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}N_{0}\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{N_{0}vv}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}v}

The last
\beta
-reduction follows from the aforementioned consequence of the induction hypothesis, namely
N_{0}v\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}M_{0}
.

•

Second suppose that
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a variable but not
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Suppose in particular that
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is the variable
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. By this distinctness and
B
being regular by the hypothesis of the theorem, the following is a Cardinal term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
(cf. distinctness in the definition of Cardinal term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
in Definition
4.1
):

{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}\equiv\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow C\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xvy}}}

(21)

Since
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a variable of state type
C
which is distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, it follows that the first and third bound variables of
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}
are not free in
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
. Further
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is free for
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
in
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xvy}
. Then we have the first step in the following, which is a
\beta
-reduction of distance 1:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}N_{0}M_{1}\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\big(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow C\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xvM_{1}}}\big)N_{0}

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{N_{0}vM_{1}}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}M_{1}}

(22)

The second
\beta
-reduction follows since
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is not free in
N_{0}
, and since the displayed free occurrence of
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow C\rightarrow B
is the only one since
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is of state type. The third
\beta
-reduction follows from the aforementioned consequence of the induction hypothesis, namely
N_{0}v\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}M_{0}
.

•

Third suppose that
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a constant
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
. Then the following is a Dardinal term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
:

{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,C,B}\equiv\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow C\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xvc}}

Since
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is not free in
N_{0}
, we have that
N_{0}
