is free for
x
in
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xvc}
, and so we have the first
\beta
-reduction in the following:

{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,C,B}N_{0}\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{N_{0}vc}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}c}

The second
\beta
-reduction follows from the aforementioned consequence of the induction hypothesis, namely
N_{0}v\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}M_{0}
.

Corollary 34
.

Every term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
is expanded
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

Proof 4.8
.

This is by induction on complexity of term. A variable or constant is itself
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial. The inductive step for application is trivial. The inductive step for lambda abstraction follows from the previous theorem.

See
5

Proof 4.9
.

The necessity is obvious. For sufficiency, suppose that
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a term. We must show that its denotation is well-defined in
\mathcal{M}
. By the previous Corollary, there is
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
such that
N\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}M
. By the hypothesis, the denotation of
N
is well-defined in
\mathcal{M}
. By Proposition
16
, the denotation of
M
is well-defined in
\mathcal{M}
.

4.4.
Semantic conservation and expressibility

See
1

Proof 4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a model of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. By Theorem
5
, it suffices to ensure that the denotations of all instances of
\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D},\mathsf{K},\mathsf{W}
which are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{M}
. By inspection of
\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D},\mathsf{K},\mathsf{W}
in Definition
4.1
the only instance we need to check is the following instance of Cardinal where
C
is a regular type and
B
is a state type and
j>0
:

{\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}\equiv\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{j}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{vv_{j}v_{0}}}}

(23)

If
\theta(B)>1
, then we are done since we can
\alpha
-convert
v_{j}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
to
v_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
. Henceforth assume
\theta(B)=1
. Without loss of generality, we can consider ourselves to be in the case where
\mathcal{M}
is a model of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
.

Consider the following closed term
M
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
:

\displaystyle M\hskip 5.69054pt\equiv\hskip 5.69054pt

\displaystyle\lambda v:B\rightarrow B\rightarrow C.\;\;\lambda v_{0}:B

(24)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt{\bigg(\lambda{V}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{(B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{V(vv_{0})}}\bigg)\big(\lambda{U}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uv_{0}}\big)}

Since
\mathcal{M}
is a model of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
, one has that the denotation of
M
is well-defined in
\mathcal{M}
.

As in the discussion of Example
2.6
, one cannot do
\beta
-reduction in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
directly on
M
to get
{\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}
, since the term
\lambda{U}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B\rightarrow C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uv_{0}}
is not free for
V
in
\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(V(vv_{0}))}
; and one cannot do
\alpha
-conversion to convert
\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(V(vv_{0}))}
to
\lambda{v_{j}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(V(vv_{j}))}
since
v_{0}
is the only variable of type
B
when
\theta(B)=1
.

However, the semantics for lambda abstraction are given by lambda abstraction out in the metatheory (cf. discussion subsequent to Definition
3.1
). In particular, the denotation
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}
in
\mathcal{M}
of the closed term
M
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
is equal to the following:

\displaystyle\Lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{M}(B\rightarrow B\rightarrow C)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\Lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{M}(B)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\bigg(\Lambda{V}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{(\mathcal{M}((B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow C)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\Lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{M}(B)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{V(vv_{0})}}\bigg)\big(\Lambda{U}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{M}(B\rightarrow C)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uv_{0}}\big)

Since one can do
\alpha
-conversion out in the metatheory, one can
\alpha
-convert the meta-term
\Lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{M}(B)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(V(vv_{0}))}
to
\Lambda{v_{j}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{M}(B)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(V(vv_{j}))}
for some
j>0
; and then by
\beta
-reduction out in the metatheory, following the reduction given in Example
2.6
, one can get the denotation
\llbracket{\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M}}
of the closed term
{\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
.

We can similarly establish:

See
2

Proof 4.11
.

We fix a signature which contains exactly the constants of
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. By Corollary
34
applied to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, one has that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is expanded
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, and hence there is
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
with the same free variables as
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
such that
L\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{\omega}N
. Let
\mathcal{M}
be a model of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. By Proposition
16
, one has that
\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}
for all variable assignments
\rho
relative to
\mathcal{M}
.

By the same argument as the previous theorem, there is term
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with the same free variables as
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
such that
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}
for all variable assignments
\rho
relative to
\mathcal{M}
. Further, this choice of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does not depend on
\mathcal{M}
: one simply uniformly replaces (
23
) in
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
with (
24
) to form
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. By the previous paragraph, we then have that
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket L\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}=\llbracket N\rrbracket_{\mathcal{M},\rho}
for all variable assignments
\rho
relative to
\mathcal{M}
.

Since the free variables of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are the same and are in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, and since we have fixed a signature throughout, we are done.

4.5.
Deductive completeness and conservation

In the previous section, we discussed semantic notions like semantic conservation and semantic expressibility. We turn now to deductive matters.

As described in §
1
, we define the
\infty
-rule in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
as follows:

{defi}
[
\infty
-rule]
Suppose that
M,N:A
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Then one defines the reduction relation
\infty
, called the
\infty
-rule
, by
M\rightarrow_{\infty}^{\theta}N
iff
M=_{\beta\eta}^{\omega}N
.

Since
\beta\eta
-equality is compatible (cf. §
2.4
), the
\infty
-rule is itself compatible. One then has:

Theorem 35

(Deductive conservation of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
over
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with
\infty
-rule)
.

For terms
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, one has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
iff
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\beta\eta\infty}M=N
.

Proof 4.12
.

Suppose
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

First suppose
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
. Then
M=_{\beta\eta}^{\omega}N
. By definition,
M\rightarrow_{\infty}^{\theta}N
. Then
M\rightarrow_{\beta\eta\infty}^{\theta}N
.

Second suppose that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\beta\eta\infty}M=N
. Let
M_{0},\ldots,M_{n}
be terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
such that
M
is
M_{0}
and
N
is
M_{n}
for some
n\geq 1
such that for each
i<n
one has that
M_{i}\rightarrow_{\gamma}^{\theta}M_{i+1}
or
M_{i+1}\rightarrow_{\gamma}^{\theta}M_{i}
for some
\gamma
in
\{\beta,\eta,\infty\}
. In the case of
\gamma
in
\{\beta,\eta\}
we continue to have
M_{i}\rightarrow_{\gamma}^{\omega}M_{i+1}
or
M_{i+1}\rightarrow_{\gamma}^{\omega}M_{i}
and in the case of
\gamma=\infty
we have
M_{i}=_{\beta\eta}^{\omega}M_{i+1}
by definition of the
\infty
-rule and the aforementioned fact that it is compatible. Hence the chain is also a chain of
\beta\eta
equalities in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, and so we have
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
.

Further one has:

See
6

Proof 4.13
.

Suppose
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. By Theorem
35
, one has that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\beta\eta\infty}M=N
iff
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
, which by the Completeness Theorem for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
(Theorem
20
) happens iff
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\models M=N
, which by the semantic conservation result (Theorem
1
) happens iff
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\models M=N
.

Note further that deductive completeness, together with the semantic expressibility result (Theorem
2
) likewise entails a deductive expressibility result of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
equipped with the
\infty
-rule.

The natural question is the following:

{qu}
[Deductive conservation and completeness with just
\beta\eta
-rules]
Suppose that
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Then do the following hold?

(a)

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
iff
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
.

(b)

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
iff
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\models M=N
.

We make a couple of remarks on these questions. First, it is not hard to see that the semantic conservation result (Theorem
1
) and Completeness Theorem for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
(Theorem
20
) entails that (
a
) happens iff (
b
) happens. Second, by Theorem
33
, any failure of (
a
) can be expressed in terms of “missing” equalities between expanded
\mathsf{BCDKW}
combinatorial terms. This then recommends a study of pure
\mathsf{BCDKW}
combinatorial logic, which we make a start on in §
6
. Third, failure of (
b
) might recommend either (i) finding the an extension of the deductive system (perhaps by finding the aforementioned missing equalities), or (ii) expanding the semantics beyond the ordinary set theoretic ones considered in this paper.

Fourth and finally, our work in §
4.4
suggests a potential route to a negative resolution to Question
4.5
.

{exa}

Let
B
be a state type, and
C
a regular type. Recall the term
M
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
from equation (
24
) of the proof of Theorem
1
. Let
\kappa>1
, i.e.
\kappa=n
for some
n>1
or
\kappa=\omega
. As in Example
2.6
one has that
M\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\kappa}{\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}
, using
\beta_{0}
-reductions under a lambda abstract. Hence
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\kappa}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M={\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}
. Suppose
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB\rightarrow B\rightarrow C
is a variable of functional type. Then Cardinal
{\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}
has the behavior in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\kappa}
of taking a function
v
of two inputs and returning the function
(v_{0},v_{1})\mapsto vv_{1}v_{0}
, i.e. permuting the order of the inputs (cf. Definition
4.1
and Theorem
26
). Since permuting the order twice has the effect of returning the original function, one can check that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\kappa}\vdash_{\beta\eta}{\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}({\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}v)=v
. Then one also has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\kappa}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M(Mv)=v
. By the Soundness Theorem
17
, one also has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\kappa}\models M(Mv)=v
. Since
M
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
, by Theorem
1
we have
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}\models M(Mv)=v
.

We can then ask:

{qu}

With term
M
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
as in the previous example (also recorded in equation (
24
)), is it the case that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M(Mv)=v
?

If this negative resolution went through, then one could also consider analogous examples for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
for
n>1
which involved generalizations of Cardinal which took a function of
(n+1)
-inputs of a given state type and cyclically permuted the order of the inputs. The difficulty in completing these negative resolutions is that the usual methods for establishing non-provability are unavailable: (1) we cannot use the usual set theoretic models and the Soundness Theorem since we already know that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}\models M(Mv)=v
, and (2) Church-Rosser is in general unavailable in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
(cf. Example
2.7
) and hence one cannot convert a
\beta\eta
-equality into
\beta\eta
-reductions to a common third term. In §
6
we develop pure combinatory logic and prove the deductive conservation result of Theorem
7
, which allows us to transfer
weak

\beta
-equalities in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
to
\beta\eta
-equalities in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
. But the natural reduction
M\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta_{0}}^{\kappa}{\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}
for
\kappa>1
is not a weak
\beta_{0}
-reduction, as we noted in Example
2.6
. And the natural proof of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\kappa}\vdash_{\beta\eta}{\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}({\mathsf{C}}_{B,B,C}v)=v
uses
\eta
. Hence Theorem
7
cannot obviously be used to answer Question
4.5
in the affirmative.

5.
The simply-typed lambda calculus: modal and non-modal

In this section, we return to the relation between the simply-typed modal lambda calculus
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
and the ordinary simply-typed lambda calculus
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. Per the discussion in §
2.3
(cf. Definition
2.3
), we assume in this section that the basic entity types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
are the union of the state types and basic entity types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. As a reminder, if
A
is a state type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, then
A\rightarrow A
is a type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
but not of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
(cf. Examples
2.3
-
2.3
for concrete examples). That is, the types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
are a proper subset of the types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, and the same is true of the terms.

5.1.
Conservation and expressibility

We first prove:

See
3

Proof 5.1
.

Suppose that
A
is a type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
and suppose that
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
such that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
. By Church-Rosser for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, we have that there is a term
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
such that
M\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta\eta}L
and
N\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta\eta}L
. But since
\beta\eta
reduction preserves being a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, we have that
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is also a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, along with all the other terms in the two
\beta\eta
-chains. Then we have
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=L
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}N=L
and so
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
.

A more complicated argument shows:

See
4

Proof 5.2
.

We show by induction on length of
\beta\eta
-reduced term
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
that if the free variables and constants of
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
have types in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, then there is a term
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
with the same free variables and constants such that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
. The result then follows by using normalization for
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
to pass to a
\beta\eta
-normal form.

For length
\ell=1
, we have that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a variable or a constant, and so these must be variables of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
or constants of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
; and so we can take
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
to be
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
.

Suppose that the result holds for length
<\ell
; we show it holds for length
\leq\ell
.

First suppose that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is an application. Since each term contains at most finitely many applications, for some
k\geq 1
there are terms

N_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{k}\rightarrow A,\hskip 28.45274ptN_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{1},\ldots N_{k}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{k}

such that
N
is
N_{0}N_{1}\cdots N_{k}
and
N_{0}
is not an application. Since
N
is
\beta\eta
-reduced,
N_{0}
is not a lambda abstract. Hence,
N_{0}
is a constant or a variable. But by hypothesis this constant or variable has type in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. Then
A_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{k}\rightarrow A
is a type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. Hence
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
are types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. Further, since
N_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{1},\ldots N_{k}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{k}
are subterms of
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, we also have that their free variables and constants must be variables of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
or constants of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, and further these subterms must themselves be
\beta\eta
-reduced. Hence, by induction hypothesis applied
k
-many times to
N_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{1},\ldots N_{k}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{k}
, these are respectively expressed by
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptM_{1},\ldots M_{k}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA_{k}
, and then
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is expressed by
N_{0}M_{1}\cdots M_{k}
.

Second suppose that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a lambda abstract
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{C}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{N_{0}}
, where
N_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
, so that
A
is
C\rightarrow D
. Since
A
is a type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
, we have that
C,D
are types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. Hence
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
has type in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. Then all the free variables
N_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
have types in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. Further, since the constants of
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
and
N_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
are the same, we have that the constants of
N_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
are in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
. Finally, since
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is in
\beta\eta
-normal form, we have that
N_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is in
\beta\eta
-normal form. Hence, by induction hypothesis, we have that
N_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is expressed by
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
, and so
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is expressed by
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}
.

In the immediate sequel to this paper (
[
84
]
), we extend these results to Church’s simple theory of types. The above proof breaks down in that setting since there are object-language identities in the simple theory of types, and one can have such an identity
N_{0}=_{A}N_{1}
of type
T
(the type of truth-values), while
A
is not a type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
but only a type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.

5.2.
Application: open term models revisited

In this section, we revisit the discussion of open term models from §
3.3
. For, there it was shown that the open term applicative structure
\mathcal{O}_{1}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}
does not determine a frame, while the open term applicative structure of
\mathcal{O}_{\omega}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}
does determine a frame and indeed determines a model. Using the theorems proved thus far, we round out the discussion by showing that for
n>1
the open term applicative structure
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
with the addition of the
\infty
-rule (cf. Definition
4.5
) determines both a frame and a model.

Before doing that, we begin with the following preliminary result purely about
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
.
58
58

58

In the statement, we use the notation mentioned in footnote
35
.

Proposition 36
.

Suppose that
n>1
and that
A,B
are distinct types of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
with
A
atomic. Suppose that
B
is not of the form
C_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow C_{\ell}\rightarrow A
for any types
C_{1},\ldots,C_{\ell}
. Suppose that
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA^{n+1}\rightarrow B
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
that have no free variables of type
A
, and that
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n-1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are distinct variables which we abbreviate as
\vec{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, such that for each
i<n
one has that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M\vec{v}v_{i}=N\vec{v}v_{i}
. Then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
.

Before beginning the proof, we note that the proposition and its proof emerge rather naturally by considering the impediments to generalizing Example
3.3
to
n>1
.

Proof 5.3
.

Let
B
be
C_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow C_{\ell}\rightarrow D
, where
D
is atomic, and
D
is by hypothesis distinct from
A
. Note we may have
\ell=0
when
B
is an atomic distinct from
A
. We fix variables
y_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC_{\ell}
which we abbreviate as
\vec{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{C}
, which we suppose to be disjoint from
\vec{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. We show by induction on
m\geq 1
that if
M,N
as in the statement of the proposition have respective long normal forms
\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{\vec{y}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{C}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{UM_{1}\cdots M_{k}}}
and
\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{\vec{y}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{C}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{VN_{1}\cdots N_{k^{\prime}}}}
where
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a vector
x_{0},\ldots x_{n}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of distinct variables of length
n+1
which is disjoint from both
\vec{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
and
\vec{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{C}
, and finally where
M_{i},N_{i}
is in long normal form, and where each
M_{i},N_{i}
has length
\leq m
, then
M=_{\beta\eta}N
.
59
59

59

See
[
8
, p. 32]
and
[
79
, p. 79]
for information on long normal forms.
In this,
U,V
are variables or constants. Note that
U,V
are not of type
A
: for if
U
was of type
A
, then since
A
is atomic we would have that
k=0
and then
B
would have type
C_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow C_{\ell}\rightarrow A
, contrary to hypothesis. Further, the hypotheses imply that for each
i<n
one has:

\displaystyle(UM_{1}\cdots M_{k})[x_{0}:=v_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}=v_{n-1},x_{n}:=v_{i}]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=_{\beta\eta}(VN_{1}\cdots N_{k^{\prime}})[x_{0}:=v_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}=v_{n-1},x_{n}:=v_{i}]

By Church-Rosser, for each
i<n
, these will
\beta\eta
-reduce to a common term, and in these reductions the heads
U,V
will not change and all of the
\beta\eta
reductions will happen internal to
M_{j}[x_{0}:=v_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}=v_{n-1},x_{n}:=v_{i}]
and
N_{j}[x_{0}:=v_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}=v_{n-1},x_{n}:=v_{i}]
. This implies that
U,V
are identical and that
k^{\prime}=k
and that for each
1\leq j\leq k
and each
i<n
one has:

M_{j}[x_{0}:=v_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}=v_{n-1},x_{n}:=v_{i}]=_{\beta\eta}N_{j}[x_{0}:=v_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}=v_{n-1},x_{n}:=v_{i}]

(25)

First, we consider the base case of
m=1
. In this case, one has that each
M_{j},N_{j}
is a constant or a variable. If it is a constant or a variable of a different type than
A
or from variables of type
A
distinct from those in the vector
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, then we can conclude from (
25
) that
M_{j},N_{j}
are identical. If it is a variable of type
A
from the vector
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, then we argue that
M_{j},N_{j}
are the same variable from the vector
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Suppose that
M_{j}
is
x_{a}
and
N_{j}
is
x_{b}
, where
a,b\leq n
. Then we argue that
a=b
by considering the three possible cases:

Case 1: Suppose
a,b<n
are distinct. Then (
25
) for any
i<n
says that
v_{a}=_{\beta\eta}v_{b}
, a contradiction.

Case 2: Suppose
a<n
and
b=n
. Since
n>1
and
a<n
, we can find
i<n
with
i\neq a
. Then (
25
) for this
i<n
says that
v_{a}=_{\beta\eta}v_{i}
, a contradiction.

Case 3: Suppose
a=n
and
b<n
. This follows as in Case 2, but with the role of
a,b
reversed.

Second, we consider the induction step
m>1
. It suffices to show that
M_{j}=_{\beta\eta}N_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
. For the rest of the proof, fix
1\leq j\leq k
. Since
M_{j},N_{j}
are in long normal form, they can be written respectively as
\lambda{\vec{w}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{D}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{X\vec{P}}
and
\lambda{\vec{w}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{D}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Y\vec{Q}}
, with each
P_{i}
and each
Q_{i}
having length
<m
. If we define
P:=\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{j}}
and
Q:=\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{N_{j}}
, then these are also in long normal form. Further, (
25
) implies that
P\vec{v}v_{i}=_{\beta\eta}Q\vec{v}v_{i}
for all
i<n
. Thus by induction hypothesis applied to
P,Q
we are done.

One could not do the proof of the previous proposition in non-maximal
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
since the proof uses Church-Rosser (cf. Example
2.7
). Further, if one tried to extend the proof of the previous proposition to the case of
n=1
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, then the proof breaks down in Cases 2-3. Further, the conclusion of the previous proposition is in fact false in the case of
n=1
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, as one can see by the below example, which is closely related to Example
3.3
:

{exa}

Let
U\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA^{2}\rightarrow B
be a variable. Let
M
be the term
\lambda{v_{0}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v_{1}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uv_{0}v_{0}}}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and let
N
be
U
. Then one has
Mv_{0}v_{0}=_{\beta\eta}Nv_{0}v_{0}
but one does not have
M=_{\beta\eta}N
.

We now apply Proposition
36
to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
for
n>1
:

Proposition 37
.

Suppose that
n>1
. Suppose that
A
is a state type and
B
is a regular type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
, and that
P,Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
, and that distinct variables
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n-1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are such that for each
i<n
one has
Pv_{i}=_{\beta\eta\infty}^{n}Qv_{i}
. Then
P=_{\beta\eta\infty}^{n}Q
.

Proof 5.4
.

Since
A
is a state type and
B
is a regular type, the hypotheses on the types from Proposition
36
is satisfied. By Corollary
34
, there is a
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term
P^{\prime}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
with the same free variables as
P
such that
P^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{n}P
. Likewise, there is a
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial term
Q^{\prime}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
with the same free variables as
Q
such that
Q^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta}^{n}Q
. We abbreviate the distinct variables
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n-1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
as
\vec{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Let
M
be the term
\lambda{\vec{v}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{P^{\prime}}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
and let
N
be the term
\lambda{\vec{v}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Q^{\prime}}
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
. Then one has that
M\vec{v}v_{i}=_{\beta\eta\infty}^{n}N\vec{v}v_{i}
for each
i<n
. Then one has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M\vec{v}v_{i}=N\vec{v}v_{i}
for each
i<n
. Then by Proposition
36
one has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
. Then by Theorem
3
one has that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
. Then by Theorem
6
one has that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}\vdash_{\beta\eta\infty}M=N
. Then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}\vdash_{\beta\eta\infty}M\vec{v}=N\vec{v}
. Then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}\vdash_{\beta\eta\infty}P^{\prime}=Q^{\prime}
. Then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}\vdash_{\beta\eta\infty}P=Q
.

We then modify the definition of the open term applicative structure
\mathcal{O}_{\theta}
from Definition
3.3
so that it is defined in terms of
\beta\eta\infty
-equality rather than
\beta\eta
-equality, and let
\mathcal{O}_{\theta}^{\infty}
be this modification. Then the previous proposition implies, following the proof of Proposition
18
, that for
n>1
one has that the open term applicative structure
\mathcal{O}_{\theta}^{\infty}
determines a frame. Then one can show:

Proposition 38
.

For each
n>1
, the frame determined by the open applicative structure
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}
is a model of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
.

Proof 5.5
.

Fix
n>1
. We show that the frame associated to the open term applicative structure
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}
is a model. By Theorem
5
, it suffices to show that the denotations of all the
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatorial terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}
are well-defined in
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}
. For this, in turn, it suffices to show that this holds for the combinators. We show this for Cardinal since the others are similar. For all
P\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B\rightarrow C,Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB,R\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
one has

\llbracket xzy\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty},[x:=[P],y:=[Q],z:=[R]]}=[PRQ]=[{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQR]
by Theorem
26
. Then one has the following, where the first identity follows from the semantics for lambda abstraction (cf. Definition
3.1
(
4
)) and the last follows from the definition of the function from
\mathcal{O}^{\infty}_{n}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\infty}_{n}(C)
determined by
[{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQ]
(here we use that the open term applicative structure
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}
determines a frame), and where the middle identity follows from our previous work:

\displaystyle\llbracket\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xyz}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}^{\infty}_{n},[x:=[P],y:=[Q]]}=\Lambda{[R]}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}(A)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\llbracket xzy\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty},[x:=[P],y:=[Q]]\,[z:=[R]]}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda{[R]}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}(A)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[}{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQ][R]=[{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQ]

We use this work now for the middle identity of the next step:

\displaystyle\llbracket\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xyz}}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}^{\infty}_{n},[x:=[P]]}=\Lambda{[Q]}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}(B)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\llbracket\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty},[x:=[P]]\,[y:=[Q]]}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda{[Q]}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}(B)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[}{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P][Q]=[{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P]

And finally we use this for the middle identity of the last step, where we abbreviate
A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C
as
D
:

\displaystyle\llbracket\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{D}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xyz}}}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}^{\infty}_{n}}=\Lambda{[P]}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}(D)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\llbracket\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{z}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{xzy}}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty},[x:=[P]]}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda{[P]}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}(B)}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[}{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}][P]=[{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}]

Hence one has
\llbracket{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}\rrbracket_{\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\infty}(D)}=[{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}]
.

6.
Pure combinatory logic and the weak lambda calculus

In this section, we turn to proving Theorem
7
, which is finally established in §
6.6
.

This theorem concerns
weak

\beta
-reduction in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, designated as
\rightarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}
. Recall from the close of §
2.4
that
\rightarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}
is the smallest relation which includes
\beta
-reduction and is closed on application on both sides; and that
=_{w\beta}^{\theta}
is the smallest equivalence relation containing
\rightarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}
. Hence, these reductions and equalities are formed without assuming that they are preserved under taking of lambda abstracts.
60
60

60

That is, these equalities are formed without the
\xi
-rule (cf. footnote
37
). As mentioned in §
1
, weak systems were studied in untyped settings
by Çağman-Hindley
[
17
]
and in typed settings by Sestini
[
72
]
. But many evaluation strategies are weak, and many of them are surveyed in
[
73
]
,
[
13
]
To see the effect of this restriction, it is useful to take note of the following elementary example:

{exa}

[Paradigmatic example of a
\beta
-reduction which is not a
w\beta
-reduction]
Suppose that
A
is a regular type of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and
x,y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are distinct variables. Then one has
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{y})x}\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{x}
, but one does
not
have
\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(\lambda{y}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{y})x}\rightarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{x}
.

For another natural example, see Example
2.6
.

However, since our
\beta
-reduction notion is distanced, any instance of distance
\geq 1
contains a simplification of a lambda abstract under another lambda abstract:

{exa}
[Distanced
\beta
-reduction and weak reduction, illustrated]
Consider the following, where
A
is of state type and
B
is regular type and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
and
y\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
and
U\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B\rightarrow B
are variables:

(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uvx}})(yv)v\rightarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{Uvx})(yv)\rightarrow_{w\beta_{0}}^{\theta}Uv(yv)

(26)

We have that the first is a weak
\beta
-reduction of distance one, and the second is a weak
\beta
-reduction of distance zero, i.e. a weak
\beta_{0}
-reduction. But if we lambda abstracted over
\lambda{U}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A\rightarrow B\rightarrow B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\ldots}
in (
26
) then these would not be weak
\beta
-reductions but rather just ordinary
\beta
-reductions.

This elementary example is important because it underscores that weak
\beta
-reduction means “the closure of
\beta
-reduction (Definition
2.6
) under application on both sides” and not “no simplification of lambda abstracts under other lambda abstracts, in some more general sense of simplification.”

There is a similar moral for the equality relation:

{exa}
[Weak equality illustrated]
Suppose that
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
and
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with
B
is regular, and suppose that
M\rightarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}N
and that
M,N
are free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
L
. Then one has
L[x:=M]=_{w\beta}^{\theta}L[x:=N]
since
L[x:=M]\leftarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L})M\rightarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}(\lambda{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L})N\rightarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}L[x:=N]
. If
x
occurs free in
L
under lambda abstractions, then weak
\beta
-equality does allow some
\beta
-reduction under lambda abstracts.
61
61

61

Both Çağman-Hindley (
[
17
, Definition 4.5 p. 245]
) and Sestini (cf.
[
72
,
\sigma
-rule p. 1]
) assume that if
M\rightarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}N
then
L[x:=M]\rightarrow_{w\beta}^{\theta}L[x:=N]
. Example
6
shows that we do not need to build this in, if we are just interested in weak
\beta
-equality.

Again, the aim of this last section is to prove Theorem
7
. We prove it by developing a theory of pure intensional combinatory logic
CL_{\theta}
and by proving results which allow us to partially transfer information back and forth between weak
\beta
-equalities in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and the associated equalities in
CL_{\theta}
. The outline of this section is as follows. In §
6.1
we set up the pure combinators and their reduction relation, which also bears the traditional name of weak reduction;
62
62

62

Historically, the name for weak reduction in combinatory logic came first; then the adjective “weak” was subsequently used for the arguably correspondent notion in the lambda calculus. See Çağman-Hindley
[
17
, p. 240]
for more details of the history.
in §
6.2
we prove the Church-Rosser property for
CL_{\theta}
using Takahashi’s method of complete developments; in §
6.3
we show how to simulate abstraction using
\mathsf{BCDKW}
, which differs from the usual simulation using
\mathsf{SKI}
; in §
6.4
we show how to move from weak
\beta
-equality in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
to weak equality in
CL_{\theta}
, culminating in Theorem
63
; in §
6.5
we show how to move from weak equality in
CL_{\theta}
to
\beta\eta
equality in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, culminating in Theorem
67
. Finally, in §
6.6
we put this together to establish Theorem
7
.

6.1.
Pure typed combinators and weak reduction

The following definition simply postulates special typed constants corresponding to the typed combinatory terms from Definition
4.1
. We omit Starling and Identity bird since we can take them as defined (cf. Propositions
44
,
45
).

{defi}

[Typed combinator terms of
CL_{\theta}
]
Let
\theta
be a parameter, and let
A,B,C
be types. Then the
combinator terms of
CL_{\theta}
are

(1)

Kestrel

{\mathsf{K}}_{A,B}
which has type
A\rightarrow B\rightarrow A
. It is required that
A
has regular type.

(2)

Cardinal

{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}
which has type
(A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow B\rightarrow A\rightarrow C
. It is required that
C
has regular type and that either
A,B
are distinct types, or
A,B
are identical types with
\theta(A)=\theta(B)>1
.

(3)

Dardinal

{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}
which has type
(A\rightarrow B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow A\rightarrow C
. It is required that
C
has regular type, that
B
is a state type, and that
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a constant.

(4)

Warbler

{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}
which has type
(A\rightarrow A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow A\rightarrow B
. It is required that
B
is of regular type.

(5)

Bluebird

{\mathsf{B}}_{A,B,C}
, of type
(B\rightarrow C)\rightarrow(A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow A\rightarrow C
. It is required that
B,C
are regular types.

Each of the terms depends on the regularity of certain of their constitutive types
A,B,C
(or just
A,B
in the case of Kestrel and Warbler). But only Cardinal depends on the parameter
\theta
and will not be available in e.g.
CL_{1}
when
A,B
are identical state types. The constraint on Cardinal in (
2
) was prefigured in Remark
24
. The reason for this constraint is that we want to develop a combinatory logic that corresponds as much as possible to
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

In parallel to Definition
2.2
, we define:

{defi}
[Terms of
CL_{\theta}
]
Let
\theta
be a parameter and let a signature be fixed. Then the terms
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
CL_{\theta}
are defined as follows:

(1)

Variables
: the variables
v_{i}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
for
i<\theta(A)
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
.

(2)

Constants
: the constants
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
from the signature are terms of
CL_{\theta}
.

(3)

Combinator terms
: the combinator terms of
CL_{\theta}
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
.

(4)

Application
: If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
and
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
then the application
(MN)\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
CL_{\theta}
.

We associate application to the left, so that the term
MNL
is
(MN)L
. And we drop outer parentheses.

As with Proposition
8
, we have:

Proposition 39
.

The only terms of
CL_{\theta}
of state type are the variables and the constants.

Parallel to Theorem
26
and Proposition
28
, we define:

{defi}
[Weak reduction; redex and contractum]
We define
weak reduction

\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
to be the reduction relation on terms of
CL_{\theta}
given by the following

\displaystyle{\mathsf{K}}_{A,B}PQ\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQR\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}PRQ

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}PR\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}PRc

\displaystyle{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}PQ\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}PQQ

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathsf{B}}_{A,B,C}PQR\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P(QR)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}Pc\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P

(27)

provided that the combinatory terms are combinatory terms of
CL_{\theta}
and that the types are appropriate to make the applications well-defined (the typing will vary with the combinatory term).

We refer to terms on the left-hand side of the
\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
-arrows in (
27
) as the
redex
and we refer to the associated right-hand side as the
contractum
.

As in §
2.4
, we let
\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
be the compatible closure of the relation defined by the above schemas, i.e. the smallest binary relation on terms of
CL_{\theta}
containing the weak reductions which is closed under application on both sides. The latter means: if
P,Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
and
P\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q
then
MP\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}MQ
for all terms
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
of
CL_{\theta}
; and likewise if
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA\rightarrow B
and
M\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
then
MP\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}NP
for all terms
P\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
CL_{\theta}
.
63
63

63

As in §
2.4
, we hasten to say that “closed under application on both sides” does
not
mean: if
P\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q
and
M\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
, then
MP\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}NQ
. This would be a parallel reduction notion (cf. §
6.2
), whereas the idea described in the body of the text is explicating the idea of a single weak reduction happening somewhere inside the term.
Note that there is
no analogue
of closure under lambda abstraction in the weak reductions of combinatory logic. Finally,
\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
is the reflexive transitive closure of
\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
, while
=_{w}^{\theta}
is the smallest equivalence relation containing
\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
.

For ease of future reference, we number the following remarks:

Remark 40

(Omitting the typing in developing combinatory logic)
.

In what follows, for ease of readability, we omit explicit descriptions of the typing of combinatory terms. This is because in combinatory logic, everything is done in terms of a large number of applications, and explicitly typing all of these would excessively complicate the description of even the simplest of inferences, like the weak reductions.

Remark 41

(Remark on the Cardinal-to-Dardinal weak reduction)
.

The last weak reduction in (
27
) has, as its parallel in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, Proposition
28
rather than Theorem
26
. We call this last weak reduction, namely the weak reduction
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}Pc\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P
,
the Cardinal-to-Dardinal weak reduction
.

This is a weak reduction which is available in
CL_{n}
for
n\geq 2
but is not in general available in
CL_{1}
since, when
A,B
are identical state types, then
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}Pc
is not a term of
CL_{1}
since
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}
is not a term of
CL_{1}
. However, since Dardinal does not depend on the parameter
\theta
but does depend on the signature, we have that Dardinal is a term of
CL_{\theta}
for all parameters
\theta
when the signature has constants of state type.

6.1.1.
Appearance of variables

In
CL_{\theta}
, like in all combinatory logics, there is no primitive binding of variables, although we can later introduce a simulation thereof (cf. §
6.3
). Hence, we just speak of variables
appearing in a term
or
occurring in a term
. Terms with no variables appearing in them are called
closed
.

We use
L[x:=N]
for the result of substituting all occurrences of variable
x\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
by term
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in term
L
. If
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
is a pairwise distinct set of variables, then we use
L[\vec{x}:=\vec{N}]
for the result of simultaneously substituting, in term
L
, all occurrences any variable in the vector
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
by the corresponding term in
\vec{N}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{A}
. The substitution lemma then reads as follows:

Lemma 42

(Substitution Lemma)
.

Suppose
P\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q
. Then:

(1)

The variables appearing in
Q
are a subset of the variables appearing in
P
.

(2)

R[v:=P]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R[v:=Q]

(3)

P[\vec{x}:=\vec{N}]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q[\vec{x}:=\vec{N}]

Proof 6.1
.

The proof is identical to
[
47
, Lemma 2.14 p. 25]
.

6.1.2.
Combinatory logic and the partial order on parameters

Recall from §
2.1
the natural partial order on parameters:
\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}
iff for all state types
A
one has
\theta(A)\leq\theta^{\prime}(A)
.

Obviously given the definition of terms of
CL_{\theta}
in Definition
6.1
, we have that if
\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}
, then all terms of
CL_{\theta}
are terms of
CL_{\theta^{\prime}}
. Further often it is a proper subset. For example, if
A,B
are identical state types with
\theta(A)=\theta(B)=1
, then
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}
is not a term of
CL_{\theta}
, but it would be a term of
CL_{\theta^{\prime}}
for any
\theta^{\prime}>\theta
with
\theta^{\prime}(A)=\theta^{\prime}(B)>1
.

Lemma 43

(Weak reduction preserves
CL_{\theta}
)
.

Suppose that
\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}
.

Suppose that
P\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a term of
CL_{\theta}
, and suppose
Q\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a term of
CL_{\theta^{\prime}}
.

If
P\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q
then
Q
is also a term of
CL_{\theta}
.

Proof 6.2
.

It suffices to show it for
\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
. But this follows by inspection of Definition
6.1
: for we see that the contractum is formed by
\leq 3
applications to subterms of the redex. The only exception to this is the Cardinal-to-Dardinal weak reduction (cf. Remark
41
), which additionally includes a new Dardinal term in the contractum. But since the Dardinal terms do not depend on the parameter it too is a term of
CL_{\theta}
.

6.1.3.
Recovery of other combinators

In parallel to Proposition
29
we have:

Proposition 44

(Recovery of Starling)
.

Suppose
A,B,C
are types and
A,B
are regular. Then there is a closed term
{\mathsf{S}}_{A,B,C}
of
CL_{\theta}
of type
(C\rightarrow A\rightarrow B)\rightarrow(C\rightarrow A)\rightarrow C\rightarrow B
such that
{\mathsf{S}}_{A,B,C}PQR\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}PR(QR)
for all terms
P,Q,R
of
CL_{\theta}
of the appropriate type to make the applications well-formed.

Proof 6.3
.

In
CL_{\theta}
, we may take
{\mathsf{S}}_{A,B,C}
to be the following term, where
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i}
are defined in terms of
A,B,C
as the proof of Proposition
29
:

{\mathsf{B}}_{A_{1},B_{1},C_{1}}({\mathsf{B}}_{A_{2},B_{2},C_{2}}({\mathsf{B}}_{A_{3},B_{3},C_{3}}{\mathsf{W}}_{A_{4},B_{4}}){\mathsf{C}}_{A_{5},B_{5},C_{5}})({\mathsf{B}}_{A_{6},B_{6},C_{6}}{\mathsf{B}}_{A_{7},B_{7},C_{7}})

Then just use weak reductions.

In parallel to Proposition
30
we have:

Proposition 45

(Recovery of Identity)
.

Suppose
B
is a regular type. Then there is a closed term
{\mathsf{I}}_{B}
of
CL_{\theta}
of type
B\rightarrow B
such that
{\mathsf{I}}_{B}P\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P
for all terms
P\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
of
CL_{\theta}
.

Proof 6.4
.

Again we use
{\mathsf{S}}_{B\rightarrow B,B,B}{\mathsf{K}}_{B,B\rightarrow B}{\mathsf{K}}_{B,B}
.

6.2.
Church-Rosser

In this subsection, we prove Church-Rosser for weak reduction in
CL_{\theta}
(Theorem
52
). The proof follows closely the outline of Takahashi’s proof of Church-Rosser for the untyped lambda calculus.
64
64

64

[
80
]
. See
[
18
, §7.2]
for discussion of the history of related proofs of Church-Rosser.

It begins with a parallel reduction notion. As its name suggests, it is trying to isolate a notion where multiple weak reductions are happening simultaneously.

{defi}
[Parallel reduction]
The binary relation
\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
is the least binary relation on terms of
CL_{\theta}
of the same type
which satisfies:

(1)

P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P
whenever
P
is a variable, constant, or combinatory term.

(2)

If
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q^{\prime}
and
R\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R^{\prime}
, then

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt{\mathsf{K}}_{A,B}PQ\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQR\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}R^{\prime}Q^{\prime}

\displaystyle{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}PR\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}R^{\prime}c

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}PQ\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}Q^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt{\mathsf{B}}_{A,B,C}PQR\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}(Q^{\prime}R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}Pc\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P^{\prime}

provided that the combinatory terms are combinatory terms of
CL_{\theta}
and that the types are appropriate to make the applications well-defined (the typing will vary with the combinatory term).

(3)

If
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q^{\prime}
, then
PQ\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}
, provided that the types are appropriate to make the applications well-defined.

As with many inductive definitions, it can be built up from below:

Proposition 46

(Characterisation of parallel reduction “from below”)
.

The definition of
\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
in Definition
6.2
is equivalent to the union of
\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}
where we define this recursively in
s\geq 0
:

(1)

For stage
s=0
, the relation
\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}
is the identity relation on variables, constants, and combinatory terms.

(2)

For even stages
s\geq 0
, the relation
\Rightarrow_{w,s+1}^{\theta}
is the union of the previous stages plus
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}PQ\Rightarrow_{w,s+1}^{\theta}P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}Q^{\prime}
for all
P\Rightarrow_{w,r}^{\theta}P^{\prime}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w,t}^{\theta}Q^{\prime}
with
r,t\leq s
; and similarly for the other weak reductions.

(3)

For odd stages
s\geq 0
, the relation
\Rightarrow_{w,s+1}^{\theta}
is the union of the previous stages plus
PQ\Rightarrow_{w,s+1}^{\theta}P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}
for all
P\Rightarrow_{w,r}^{\theta}P^{\prime}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w,t}^{\theta}Q^{\prime}
with
r,t\leq s
.

The proof is standard and so we omit it.

The characterization “from below” can be used to show the following:

Proposition 47

(Successors of base cases under parallel reduction; successors of non-redexes under parallel reduction)
.

(1)

If
M
is a variable, constant, or combinatory term and
M\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
then
N
is
M
.

(2)

If
M
is an application
PQ
which is not a redex and
M\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
, then
N
is
P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}
where
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q^{\prime}
.

Proof 6.5
.

For (
1
), we show by induction on
s\geq 0
that if
M
is a variable, constant, or combinatory term and
M\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}N
then
N
is
M
:

•

For
s=0
, if we add
M\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}N
, then
M
is a variable, constant, or combinatory term and
N
is
M
.

•

At stage
s+1
, we do not add any parallel reductions
M\Rightarrow_{w,s+1}^{\theta}N
with
M
a variable, constant, or combinatory term; hence we are done by induction hypothesis.

In this argument and subsequent inductive arguments, we use “add at a stage” to mean that it is in the stage but not in any of the previous stages.

For (
2
), we show by induction on
s\geq 0
that if
M
is an application
PQ
which is not a redex and
M\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}N
, then
N
is
P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}
where
P\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}P^{\prime}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}Q^{\prime}
:

•

For
s=0
, we do not add any parallel reductions
M\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}N
where
M
is an application.

•

At stage
s+1
with
s
even, we do not add any parallel reductions
M\Rightarrow_{w,s+1}^{\theta}N
where
M
is an application which is not a redex; and hence we are done by induction hypothesis.

•

At stage
s+1
with
s
odd, if we add a parallel reduction
M\Rightarrow_{w,s+1}^{\theta}N
where
M
is an application
PQ
which is not a redex, then
N
is
P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}
where
P\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}P^{\prime}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}Q^{\prime}
.

Using simple inductive proofs which we omit, one can also identify the successors of the other combinators under parallel reduction:

Proposition 48

(Successors of Warblers under parallel reduction)
.

(1)

If
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
, then
N
is identical to
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}
.

(2)

If
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
, then
N
is identical to
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}P_{1}
for some term
P_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
.

(3)

If
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}PQ\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
, then one of the following occurs:

(a)

N
is
P_{1}Q_{1}Q_{1}
for some terms
P_{1},Q_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
.

(b)

N
is
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}P_{1}Q_{1}
for some terms
P_{1},Q_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
.

There are analogous propositions for the other “two input” combinatory terms of Kestrel and Dardinal, with the only difference being that one modifies (
3
3a
) appropriately.

For the “three input” combinatory term of Cardinal, one has:

Proposition 49

(Successors of Cardinals under parallel reduction)
.

(1)

If
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
, then
N
is identical to
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}
.

(2)

If
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
, then
N
is identical to
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P_{1}
for some term
P_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
.

(3)

If
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQ\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
, then one of the following occurs:

(a)

N
is identical to
{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P_{1}
for some constant
c
and some term
P_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
; further
Q
is identical to
c
.

(b)

N
is identical to
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P_{1}Q_{1}
for some term
P_{1},Q_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
.

(4)

If
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQR\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
, then one of the following occurs:

(a)

N
is identical to
P_{1}R_{1}Q_{1}
for some terms
P_{1},Q_{1},R_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
and
R\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R_{1}
.

(b)

N
is identical to
{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P_{1}R_{1}{}
for some constant
c
and some terms
P_{1},R_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
R\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R_{1}
; further
Q
is identical to
c
.

(c)

N
is identical to
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P_{1}Q_{1}R_{1}
for some terms
P_{1},Q_{1},R_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
and
R\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R_{1}
.

There is an analogous proposition for Bluebird, but is simpler in that clauses (
3
3a
) and (
4
4b
) can be omitted. And of course to obtain the analogous proposition for Bluebird, one modifies (
4
4a
) appropriately.

The following lemma is important because (
2
)-(
3
) imply that weak reduction and parallel reduction have the same transitive closure (cf.
[
80
, p. 120 equations (1)-(3)]
):

Lemma 50
.

(1)

P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P

(2)

If
P\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q
then
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q

(3)

If
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q
then
P\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q

(4)

If
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q^{\prime}
then
P[x:=Q]\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}[x:=Q^{\prime}]

Proof 6.6
.

For (
1
), this follows from an easy induction on complexity of
P
from Definition
6.2
(
1
), (
3
).

For (
2
), simply use (
1
) and Definition
6.2
(
2
) to handle the case when the reduction happens at the top level, and then use (
1
) and Definition
6.2
(
3
) to handle when the reduction happens embedded inside applications.

For (
3
) use induction on
s\geq 0
to show that
P\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}Q
implies
P\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q
.

For (
4
) we use an induction on
s\geq 0
to show that
P\Rightarrow_{w,s}^{\theta}P^{\prime}
implies that for all
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q^{\prime}
we have
P[x:=Q]\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\prime}[x:=Q^{\prime}]
.

Takahashi’s concept complement development is, in our
CL_{\theta}
, the following (cf.

[
80
, p. 121]
):

{defi}
[The complete development]
The
complete development

M^{\ast}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
of a term
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
of
CL_{\theta}
is defined by induction on complexity of term as follows:

(1)

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a variable, constant, or combinatory term, then
M^{\ast}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
.

(2)

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is an application
PQ\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
which is not a redex, then we define
M^{\ast}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
to be
P^{\ast}Q^{\ast}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
.

(3)

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a redex, then we define
M^{\ast}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
as follows:

M^{\ast}=\begin{cases}P^{\ast}&\text{if $M$ is ${\mathsf{K}}_{A,B}PQ$},\\
P^{\ast}R^{\ast}Q^{\ast}&\text{if $M$ is ${\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQR$},\\
P^{\ast}R^{\ast}c&\text{if $M$ is ${\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}PR$},\\
P^{\ast}Q^{\ast}Q^{\ast}&\text{if $M$ is ${\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}PQ$},\\
P^{\ast}(Q^{\ast}R^{\ast})&\text{if $M$ is ${\mathsf{B}}_{A,B,C}PQR$}\\
{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P^{\ast}{}&\text{if $M$ is ${\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}Pc$},\\
\end{cases}

The Takahashi proof of Church-Rosser then goes through the following proposition (cf.
[
80
, p. 121]
):

Proposition 51
.

If
M\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
then
N\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M^{\ast}
.

Proof 6.7
.

This is by induction on complexity of
M
.

First suppose that
M
is a variable, constant, or combinatory term. Suppose
M\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
. Then Proposition
47
(
1
) we have that
N
is
M
. We are then done since by Definition
6.2
(
1
), we have that
M^{\ast}
is also
M
.

Second suppose that
M
is an application
PQ
which is not a redex. Suppose
M\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
. Then Proposition
47
(
2
), we have that
N
is
P_{1}Q_{1}
where
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
. By induction hypothesis

P_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}
and
Q_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q^{\ast}
. Then by Definition
6.2
(
3
), we have
P_{1}Q_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}Q^{\ast}
, which by Definition
6.2
(
2
) is equal to
(PQ)^{\ast}
.

Third suppose that
M
is a redex.

First consider the Warbler case where
M
is
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}PQ
. Suppose
M\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
. By Proposition
48
(
3
), there are two cases to consider:

•

First suppose
N
is
P_{1}Q_{1}Q_{1}
where
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
. By induction hypothesis

P_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}
and
Q_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q^{\ast}
. Then by two applications of Definition
6.2
(
3
), we have
P_{1}Q_{1}Q_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}Q^{\ast}Q^{\ast}
, which by Definition
6.2
(
3
) is equal to
({\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}PQ)^{\ast}
.

•

Second suppose
N
is
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}P_{1}Q_{1}
where
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
. By induction hypothesis

P_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}
and
Q_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q^{\ast}
. By an application of Definition
6.2
(
2
), we have
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}P_{1}Q_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}Q^{\ast}Q^{\ast}
which by Definition
6.2
(
3
) is equal to
({\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}PQ)^{\ast}
.

The proofs for the other “two input” combinatory terms of Kestrel and Dardinal are entirely identical.

Second consider the Cardinal case. There are two Cardinal redexes and so two subcases.

First consider subcase where
M
is the redex
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}Pc
. Suppose
M\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
. By Proposition
49
(
3
) (with
Q
in that proposition set identical to
c
), there are two subcases to consider:

•

N
is identical to
{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P_{1}
for some term
P_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
. Then by induction hypothesis,
P_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}
. Then by an application of Definition
6.2
(
1
),(
3
), we have
{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P^{\ast}
, and the latter is is equal to
({\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}Pc)^{\ast}
by Definition
6.2
(
3
).

•

N
is identical to
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P_{1}Q_{1}
for some
P_{1},Q_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
c\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
. By Proposition
47
(
1
) we have that
Q_{1}
is
c
. Further, by induction hypothesis,
P_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}
. Then by Definition
6.2
(
2
) we have
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P_{1}c\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P^{\ast}
, and the latter is is equal to
({\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}Pc)^{\ast}
by Definition
6.2
(
3
).

Second consider the subcase where
M
is the redex
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQR
. Suppose
M\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
. By Proposition
49
(
4
), there are three subcases to consider:

•

N
is identical to
P_{1}R_{1}Q_{1}
for some
P_{1},Q_{1},R_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
and
R\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R_{1}
. Then by induction hypothesis,
P_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}
and
Q_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q^{\ast}
and
R_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R^{\ast}
. Then by two applications of Definition
6.2
(
3
), we have
P_{1}R_{1}Q_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}R^{\ast}Q^{\ast}
, and the latter is is equal to
({\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQR)^{\ast}
by Definition
6.2
(
3
).

•

N
is identical to
{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P_{1}R_{1}{}
for constant
c
and some terms
P_{1},R_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
R\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R_{1}
; further
Q
is identical to
c
. Then by induction hypothesis,
P_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}
and
R_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R^{\ast}
. By Definition
6.2
(
1
),
Q^{\ast}
is also identical to
c
.
By Definition
6.2
(
2
) we have
{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,B,C}P_{1}R_{1}{}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}R^{\ast}c
, which is the same term as
P^{\ast}R^{\ast}Q^{\ast}
, and the latter is is equal to
({\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQR)^{\ast}
by Definition
6.2
(
3
).

•

N
is identical to
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P_{1}Q_{1}R_{1}
for some
P_{1},Q_{1},R_{1}
such that
P\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P_{1}
and
Q\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q_{1}
and
R\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R_{1}
. Then by induction hypothesis,
P_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}
and
Q_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}Q^{\ast}
and
R_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}R^{\ast}
. Then by Definition
6.2
(
2
), we have
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}P_{1}Q_{1}R_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}P^{\ast}R^{\ast}Q^{\ast}
, and the latter is is equal to
({\mathsf{C}}_{A,B,C}PQR)^{\ast}
by Definition
6.2
(
3
).

The other “three input” case of Bluebird is similar, but simpler.

Theorem 52

(Church-Rosser for weak reduction in
CL_{\theta}
)
.

Suppose that
M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
such that
M_{1}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M_{2}
and
M_{1}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M_{3}
. Then there is a term
M_{4}
of
CL_{\theta}
such that
M_{2}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M_{4}
and
M_{3}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M_{4}
.

Proof 6.8
.

The previous theorem implies that if
M\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N_{0}
and
M\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N_{1}
, then
N_{0}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M^{\ast}
and
N_{1}\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M^{\ast}
. By a classic diagram chase argument, this implies that the transitive closure of
\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
has the Church-Rosser property (cf.
[
9
, Lemma 3.2.2 p. 59]
). Then we are done by Proposition
50
(
2
)-(
3
), which implies that the transitive closure of
\Rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
is the same as
\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
.

A traditional proof then also gives (cf.
[
9
, Theorem 3.1.12 p. 54]
):

Corollary 53

(Church-Rosser and weak equality in
CL_{\theta}
)
.

Suppose that
M_{1},M_{2}
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
such that
M_{1}=_{w}^{\theta}M_{2}
.

Then there is a term
M_{3}
of
CL_{\theta}
such that
M_{1}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M_{3}
and
M_{2}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M_{3}
.

From this we can derive:

Corollary 54

(Conservation of
CL_{\omega}
over
CL_{\theta}
)
.

Suppose that
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
.

Then
CL_{\theta}\vdash_{w}M=N
iff
CL_{\omega}\vdash_{w}M=N
.

Proof 6.9
.

The forward direction is trivial since any
\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
reduction is a
\rightarrow_{w}^{\omega}
reduction.

Conversely, suppose that
CL_{\omega}\vdash_{w}M=N
. Then by definition
M=_{w}^{\omega}N
. By Corollary
53
, there is a term
L
of
CL_{\omega}
such that
M\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\omega}L
and
N\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\omega}L
. But since
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
, by Lemma
43
so also we have that
L
and everything else in the two
\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\omega}
-chains are terms of
CL_{\theta}
. Hence we also have
M=_{w}^{\theta}N
, which by definition means
CL_{\theta}\vdash_{w}M=N
.

6.3.
Simulating abstraction

Despite its lack of a primitive binding apparatus, combinatory logic famously allows one to simulate core aspects of lambda abstraction. The usual definition of this goes through the combinatory terms
\mathsf{SKI}
, but the proof of Theorem
33
suggests the following definition which deploys
\mathsf{BCDKW}
. While there is some suggestion that Curry did something like this in his early work, this definition is to my knowledge new.
65
65

65

See
[
18
, §5]
. In Curry
[
25
, p. 238]
one finds a description of why
\mathsf{BCKW}
should suffice, but the more formal discussion in Curry
[
25
, p. 190]
(list at the bottom) does not include Warbler, and does not contain the case breaks I have used.

{defi}

[Combinatory abstraction]
Suppose
A,B
are types and
B
is a regular type and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a variable of
CL_{\theta}
and
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
CL_{\theta}
. Then, by induction on complexity of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
, we define term
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
of type
A\rightarrow B
of
CL_{\theta}
as follows:

(1)

If
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does not appear in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
, then we define
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
to be
{\mathsf{K}}_{B,A}M
.

(2)

If
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does appear in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
and
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, then we define
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
to be
{\mathsf{I}}_{A}
.

(3)

If
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does appear in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
and
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
M_{0}M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
where
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC\rightarrow B
and
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
, then we define

(a)

If
C
is a state type then

[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}M_{1}}=\begin{cases}{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)&\text{if $M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC$ is $v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA$},\\
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)M_{1}{}&\text{if $M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC$ is a variable but not $v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA$},\\
{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,C,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big){}{}&\text{if $M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC$ is the constant $c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC$},\\
\end{cases}

(b)

If
C
is a regular type, then

[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}M_{1}}\;=\;{\mathsf{S}}_{C,B,A}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{1}}\big)

Regarding Definition
6.3
(
3
3a
), note that the hypothesis that
C
is a state type and the second case break hypothesis that
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a variable but not
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
has the consequence that either
A,C
are distinct types, or
A,C
are identical types and
\theta(A)=\theta(C)>1
. This, in conjunction with
B
being regular implies that Cardinal
{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}
is a term of
CL_{\theta}
by Definition
6.1
.

Definition
6.3
is a definition by induction on complexity of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
. In particular, Definition
6.3
(
1
)-(
2
) cover the cases of variables, constants, and combinatory terms; and supposing it has been defined for
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC\rightarrow B
and
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
, we define it for
M_{0}M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
by breaking into cases according to Definition
6.3
(
1
), (
3
), where we appeal to induction hypothesis only in the case of Definition
6.3
(
3
).

The following proposition is a technical one. One can see it as simultaneously accomplishing two things: showing an elementary instance of the analogue of
\beta
-reduction is available, and then identifying the variables appearing in a combinatory abstract (cf.
[
47
, p. 27]
).

Proposition 55

(Elementary instance of analogue of
\beta
-reduction; variables appearing in an combinatory abstract)
.

Suppose
A,B
are types and
B
is a regular type and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a variable of
CL_{\theta}
and
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
CL_{\theta}
. Then

(1)

([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})v\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M
.

(2)

The variables appearing in
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
are precisely those appearing in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
minus
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
.
In particular,
v
does not appear in
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
.

Proof 6.10
.

We argue by induction on complexity of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
.

The most interesting case is the induction step where
v
appears in
M
and
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is the application
M_{0}M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
with
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC\rightarrow B
and
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
and
C
being a state type. There are three subcases, corresponding to the three subcases of Definition
6.3
(
3
3a
). Since they are similar we only do one of them.

Suppose that
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Then
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}M_{1}}
is
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)
. By induction hypothesis for
M_{0}
, the variables appearing in
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)
are precisely those appearing in
M_{0}
minus
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
; and due to
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
being
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, this is equal to the those appearing in
M_{0}M_{1}
minus
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Further, by Definition
6.1
one has
{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)v\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)vv\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}M_{0}v
, where the second weak reduction is by induction hypothesis for
M_{0}
.

The following technical proposition is the combinatory analogue of the lambda calculus identity
\big(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)[u:=N]\equiv\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M[u:=N]\big)}
, when variables
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA,u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
are distinct and
v
does not occur free in
N
. It is more complicated in combinatory logic simply because the combinatory abstracts are defined via a large number of case breaks. Further, unlike the usual
\mathsf{SKI}
-combinatory logic, we get a weak equality in our
\mathsf{BCDKW}
-combinatory logic rather than a literal identity of terms, unless we restrict to a signature with no constants of state type (cf.
[
47
, Lemma 2.28 (c) p. 29]
). We use this proposition in the proof of Proposition
62
, which in turn is used in the proof of Theorem
63
.

Proposition 56

(Substituting in a combinatory abstract)
.

Suppose
A,B
are types and
B
is a regular type and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a variable of
CL_{\theta}
and
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
CL_{\theta}
.

Suppose
D
is a type and
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is a variable of
CL_{\theta}
distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, and suppose that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is a term of
CL_{\theta}
in which
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does not appear.

Then:

(1)

In a signature with constants of state type,
\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)[u:=N]=_{w}^{\theta}[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M[u:=N]\big)}
.

(2)

In a signature with no constants of state type,
\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)[u:=N]\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M[u:=N]\big)}
.

Proof 6.11
.

For (
1
), this proof is by induction on complexity of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
, with a universal quantifier over
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
,
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
.

Suppose that
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does not appear in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
. Since
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does not appear in
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
, we have that
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does not appear in
M[u:=N]
. Then we have
([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})[u:=N]\equiv\big({\mathsf{K}}_{B,A}M\big)[u:=N]\equiv{\mathsf{K}}_{B,A}\big(M[u:=N]\big)\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M[u:=N]\big)}
, wherein the first and the last are by two applications of Definition
6.3
(
1
).

Suppose that
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does appear in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
and that
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Then by Proposition
55
(
2
), no variables appear in
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
, and so
\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)[u:=N]
is
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
. Further, since
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, we have that
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M[u:=N]\big)}
is also
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
. By Definition
6.3
(
2
), both terms are identical to
{\mathsf{I}}_{A}
.

Suppose for the remainder of the proof that
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does appear in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
and
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
M_{0}M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
where
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC\rightarrow B
and
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
. Since
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA,u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
are distinct variables,
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
appears in
M[u:=N]
; and the term
M[u:=N]
is
M_{0}[u:=N]M_{1}[u:=N]
. By induction hypothesis, we have that the result holds for
M_{0}
and that it holds for
M_{1}
when
C
is of regular type.

As a first case, suppose that
C
is a state type. There are then four subcases.

First suppose that
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Since
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA,u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
are distinct variables, we also have that
M_{1}[u:=N]
is
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Then we have the following:

\displaystyle\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}M_{1}}\big)[u:=N]\equiv\big({\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)\big)[u:=N]
