\displaystyle\equiv{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}\big(\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)[u:=N]\big)=_{w}^{\theta}{\mathsf{W}}_{A,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M_{0}[u:=N]\big)}\big)

\displaystyle\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M_{0}[u:=N]v\big)}\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M_{0}[u:=N]M_{1}[u:=N]\big)}

\displaystyle\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big((M_{0}M_{1})[u:=N]\big)}

In this, the first and third-to-last are by two applications of the Warbler case of Definition
6.3
(
3
3a
), and the one with
=_{w}^{\theta}
follows from induction hypothesis for
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC\rightarrow B
.

Second suppose that
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
. Then
D
is
C
, and hence
D
is a state type, and hence by Proposition
39
one has that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is a variable or a constant. Since
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does not appear in
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
, we have that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is a variable distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
or
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is a constant
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
.

•

First consider the subsubcase where
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is a variable but not
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Then:

\displaystyle\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}M_{1}}\big)[u:=N]\equiv\big({\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)M_{1}{}\big)[u:=N]

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}\big(\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)[u:=N]\big)M_{1}[u:=N]{}=_{w}^{\theta}{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}[u:=N]}\big)M_{1}[u:=N]{}

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}[u:=N]}\big)N{}\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M_{0}[u:=N]N\big)}

\displaystyle\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M_{0}[u:=N]M_{1}[u:=N]\big)}\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big((M_{0}M_{1})[u:=N]\big)}

In this, the first and third-to-last are by two applications of the Cardinal case of Definition
6.3
(
3
3a
), and the one with
=_{w}^{\theta}
follows from induction hypothesis for
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC\rightarrow B
.

•

Second consider the subsubcase where
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is a constant
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
. Then one has:

\displaystyle\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}M_{1}}\big)[u:=N]\equiv\big({\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)M_{1}{}\big)[u:=N]

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}\big(\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}}\big)[u:=N]\big)M_{1}[u:=N]{}=_{w}^{\theta}{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}[u:=N]}\big)M_{1}[u:=N]{}

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathsf{C}}_{A,C,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}[u:=N]}\big)N{}=_{w}^{\theta}{\mathsf{D}}^{c}_{A,C,B}\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M_{0}[u:=N]}\big)

(28)

\displaystyle\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M_{0}[u:=N]N\big)}\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M_{0}[u:=N]M_{1}[u:=N]\big)}

\displaystyle\equiv[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big((M_{0}M_{1})[u:=N]\big)}

In this, the first and third-to-last are respectively by applications of the Cardinal and Dardinal case Definition
6.3
(
3
3a
). The first line with
=_{w}^{\theta}
follows from induction hypothesis for
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC\rightarrow B
. The second line with
=_{w}^{\theta}
, namely in (
28
), follows from the Cardinal-to-Dardinal weak reduction (cf. Definition
6.1
and Remark
41
).

Since their proofs are similar, we omit the last two subcases (namely: when
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a variable but not
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
or
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
; and when
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a constant).

A second case is when
C
is a regular type. But then one uses Starling similar to how one used Warbler above.

For (
2
), the only place in the proof of (
1
) where a weak equality
=_{w}^{\theta}
rather than a syntactic identity
\equiv
occurred, outside of an induction hypothesis, was in (
28
), namely the case of where
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is a constant
c\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
of state type
C
. Hence in a signature with no constants of state type, we will have syntactic identity
\equiv
throughout.

The following proposition is the combinatory logic analogue of distanced
\beta
-equality from Definition
2.6
(cf.
[
47
, Theorem 2.21 p. 27]
). It one of the key components of the proof of the correspondence between
CL_{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
established in Theorem
63
.

Proposition 57

(The combinatory logic analogue of distanced
\beta
-equality)
.

Suppose that
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC,\vec{M}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B},N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
, and suppose that
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are variables of
CL_{\theta}
with
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B},\vec{M}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
having the same length. Suppose that

(1)

the variables in
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
do not appear in
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA

(2)

the variables in
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B},v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are pairwise distinct

Then
\big([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N=_{w}^{\theta}\big([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}
.

Proof 6.12
.

It suffices to show that both sides
\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
-reduce to
L[\vec{x}:=\vec{M},v:=N]
. Note that by condition (
2
) the simultaneous substitution in
L[\vec{x}:=\vec{M},v:=N]
is well-defined.

First we work on the left-hand side. By iterated applications of Proposition
55
(
1
), we have
([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}})\vec{x}v\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}L
. By Lemma
42
(
3
) we have
\big(\,([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}})\vec{x}v\,\big)[v:=N]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}L[v:=N]
. By Proposition
55
(
2
),
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
does not appear in
[{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}
; and by (
2
) each variable in
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
is distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Hence the left-hand side of the previous
\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
can be simplified to
([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}})\vec{x}N\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}L[v:=N]
. By Lemma
42
(
3
) again, we have
\big(\,([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}})\vec{x}N\,\big)[\vec{x}:=\vec{M}]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}\big(L[v:=N]\big)[\vec{x}:=\vec{M}]
. By Proposition
55
(
2
), the variables
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
do not appear in
[{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}
; and by (
1
) the variables
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
do not appear in
N
. Hence we can simplify on both the left and right sides of the previous
\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
as follows:
\big([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}L[\vec{x}:=\vec{M},v:=N]
.

Second we work on the right-hand side. By iterated applications of Proposition
55
(
1
), we have
\big([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{x}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}L[v:=N]
. By Lemma
42
(
3
)
\big(\,\big([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{x}\,\big)[\vec{x}:=\vec{M}]\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}\big(L[v:=N]\big)[\vec{x}:=\vec{M}]
. By Proposition
55
(
2
), the variables in
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
do not appear in
[{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}
; by (
1
) we have that the variables
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
do not appear in
N
. Hence we can simplify on both the left and right of the previous
\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}
to obtain:
\big([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}\twoheadrightarrow_{w}^{\theta}L[\vec{x}:=\vec{M},v:=N]
.

The following is an elementary observation but one which is important for later understanding the interaction of
\alpha
-conversion and combinatory logic.

Proposition 58

(Action of type-preserving permutations of variables on terms of combinatory logic)
.

Given a type-preserving permutation
\pi
of the variables of
CL_{\theta}
, we extend it to a type-preserving permutation of all terms of
CL_{\theta}
by letting it be the identity on the constants and combinatory terms, and by setting
(MN)^{\pi}
to be
M^{\pi}N^{\pi}
. Then

(1)

If
\pi
is the identity on all variables appearing in
M
, then
M^{\pi}
is
M
.

(2)

If
\pi(v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA)
is
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, then
([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{\pi}
is
[{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{\pi}}
.

Proof 6.13
.

For (
1
) this follows from construction, since
M^{\pi}
is already the identity on the constants and combinatory terms.

For (
2
) this is by induction on complexity of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
, using Definition
6.3
. We omit the proof since it is routine.

6.4.
From lambda calculus to combinatory logic

We then define a translation from terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
to terms of
CL_{\theta}
in the natural way (cf.
[
47
, Definition 9.10 p. 95]
).

{defi}

[Translation from lambda calculus to combinatory logic]
If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, then we define a term
M^{cl}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
of
CL_{\theta}
inductively as follows:

(1)

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a variable or constant, then
M^{cl}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
.

(2)

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
M_{0}M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
where
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC\rightarrow B
and
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
, then
M^{cl}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
M_{0}^{cl}M_{1}^{cl}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
.

(3)

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}
where
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
, then
M^{cl}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L^{cl}}

The following is obvious from the construction and Proposition
55
(
2
) but we include it for ease of future reference:

Proposition 59

(Free variables and appearance of variables under the translation)
.

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a variable of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, then
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
appears free in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
iff
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
appears in
M^{cl}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
.

In this next proposition, we work with type-preserving permutations of the variables of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, which recall are extended to type-preserving permutations of the terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, as part of our official definition of
\alpha
-conversion (cf. Definition
2.5
).

Proposition 60

(Commutativity of type-preserving permutations and translation)
.

For all terms
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, for all type-preserving permutations
\pi
of the variables of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, one has that
(M^{\pi})^{cl}
is
(M^{cl})^{\pi}
.

Proof 6.14
.

If
M
is a variable or constant, then both of these are equal to
M^{\pi}
.

The induction step for application is trivial.

Suppose that
\pi(v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA)
is
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Then
((\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{\pi})^{cl}
is by definition
[{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(M^{\pi})^{cl}}
, which by induction hypothesis is
[{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(M^{cl})^{\pi}}
, which by Proposition
58
(
2
) is
([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(M^{cl})})^{\pi}
, which by definition is
((\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{cl})^{\pi}
.

Since, formally, terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
are
\alpha
-equivalence classes of terms, and since the translation in Definition
6.4
is defined on members of these equivalence classes, we need to check that the translation respects the equivalence. This is so in a very strong form: “the analogue in
CL
of the
\lambda
-calculus relation of
=_{\alpha}
is simply identity” (
[
47
, p. 29]
):

Proposition 61

(Under the translation,
\alpha
-equivalence is identity)
.

(1)

Suppose
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Suppose
\pi
is a type-preserving permutation of the variables of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
which is the identity on the free variables of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
. Then
M^{cl}
is
(M^{\pi})^{cl}
.

(2)

Suppose
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
which are
\alpha
-equivalent. Then
M^{cl},N^{cl}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
which are identical.

Proof 6.15
.

For (
1
), this is by an induction on complexity of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
, with a universal quantifier over type-preserving permutations in the statement of the induction hypothesis.

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a variable, then
M^{\pi}
is
M
since by hypothesis
\pi
is the identity on the free variables of
M
; and then both
M^{cl},(M^{\pi})^{cl}
are
M
as well.

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a constant, then both
M^{cl},(M^{\pi})^{cl}
are
M
.

The induction step for application is trivial.

For the induction step for lambda abstraction
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
, suppose that
\pi(v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA)
is
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
. Then by definition,
((\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{\pi})^{cl}
is
[{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(M^{\pi})^{cl}}
. By Proposition
60
the latter is
[{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(M^{cl})^{\pi}}
. By Proposition
58
(
2
), this is identical to
([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{cl}})^{\pi}
. By definition this is identical to
((\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{cl})^{\pi}
. Since
\pi
is the identity on the free variables of
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
, by Proposition
59
we have that
\pi
is the identity on all the variables appearing in
(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{cl}
. Then by Proposition
58
(
1
), we have that
((\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{cl})^{\pi}
is identical to
(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{cl}
.

For (
2
), this follows from (
1
) and the definition of
\alpha
-equivalence, in Definition
2.5
, as the smallest equivalence relation containing the compatible closure of the relation defined in terms of permutations. The base case of the induction is handled by (
1
), and the inductive steps are trivial and so we omit them.

The following is another technical proposition (cf.
[
47
, Lemma 9.13(d) p. 97]
). We need it to establish our correspondence between
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and
CL_{\theta}
later in Theorem
63
:

Proposition 62

(Substitution in lambda calculus mirrored by substitution in combinatory logic, in a signature with no constants of state type)
.

Suppose that the signature has no constants of state type.

Suppose that
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Suppose that
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a variable of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
and suppose that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
.

If
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
, then
(L[v:=N])^{cl}\equiv L^{cl}[v:=N^{cl}]
.

Proof 6.16
.

This is by induction on complexity of term
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. The base cases and induction step for application are trivial. The interesting induction step for lambda abstraction is when
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
is
\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{D}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
, where
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
is distinct from
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, and
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
with
B
regular. Suppose that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{D}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
. Then we have that
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
M
. The non-trivial case to consider is when
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
occurs free in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
. Then since
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{D}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}
, we have that
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
does not occur free in term
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. Then by Proposition
59
we have that
u\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptD
does not appear in term
N^{cl}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
of
CL_{\theta}
. Then:

\displaystyle\big((\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{D}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})[v:=N]\big)^{cl}\equiv\big(\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{D}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M[v:=N]}\big)^{cl}\equiv[{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{D}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M[v:=N]\big)^{cl}}

\displaystyle\equiv[{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{D}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(M^{cl}[v:=N^{cl}]\big)}\equiv\big([{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{D}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{cl}}\big)[v:=N^{cl}]\equiv\big(\lambda{u}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{D}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)^{cl}[v:=N^{cl}]

On the second line, the first
\equiv
follows by induction hypothesis on
M
; and the second
\equiv
follows by Proposition
56
(
2
) and the hypothesis that we are in a signature with no constants of state type.

Theorem 63

(Weak beta equalities translate to combinatory weak equalities, in a signature with no constants of state type)
.

Suppose that the signature has no constants of state type.

Suppose that
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
. If
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{w\beta}M=N
then
CL_{\theta}\vdash_{w}M^{cl}=N^{cl}
.

Proof 6.17
.

Since the
{\cdot}^{cl}
-translation commutes with application, it suffices to show that an instance of
\beta
-reduction at the top level in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
results in a weak equality in
CL_{\theta}
under the
{\cdot}^{cl}
-translation. Suppose that we are given an instance of
\beta
-reduction in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
:

\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L}}\big)\vec{M}N\rightarrow_{\beta}^{\theta}\big(\lambda{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L[v:=N]}\big)\vec{M}

(29)

so that as in Definition
2.6
we have:

(1)

N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is free for
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
in
L\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC

(2)

the variables in
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
are not free in
N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA

(3)

the variables in
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B},v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are pairwise distinct.

By (
1
) and Proposition
62
and the definition of the
cl
-translation (cf. Definition
6.4
), it suffices to show:
\big([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{\vec{B}}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L^{cl}}}\big)\vec{M}^{cl}N^{cl}=_{w}^{\theta}\big([{\vec{x}}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{B}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{L^{cl}[v:=N^{cl}]}\big)\vec{M}^{cl}
. By Proposition
57
it suffices to show

(a)

the variables in
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B}
do not appear in
N^{cl}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA

(b)

the variables in
\vec{x}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt\vec{B},v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are pairwise distinct.

But (
a
) follows from (
2
) and Proposition
59
, while (
b
) follows directly from (
3
).

6.5.
From combinatory logic to lambda calculus

We define a second translation (cf.
[
47
, Definition 9.2 p. 93]
):

{defi}
[Translation from combinatory logic to lambda calculus]
If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
CL_{\theta}
, then we define a term
M^{\lambda}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
inductively as follows:

(1)

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a variable or constant, then
M^{\lambda}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
.

(2)

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a combinatory term from Definition
6.1
, then
M^{\lambda}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is the corresponding combinatory term
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
from Definition
4.1
.

(3)

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
M_{0}M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
where
M_{0}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC\rightarrow B
and
M_{1}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptC
, then
M^{\lambda}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is
M_{0}^{\lambda}M_{1}^{\lambda}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
.

In (
2
), we do not need to choose a specific version since they are all
\alpha
-equivalent.

We have the following analogue of Proposition
59
, which is a routine induction:

Proposition 64

(Appearance of variables and free variables under the translation)
.

If
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
is a term of
CL_{\theta}
and
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is a variable of
CL_{\theta}
, then
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
appears in
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
iff
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
appears free in
M^{\lambda}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
.

In the next results, we further pay attention to regular
\beta
-reductions (cf. Definition
2.6
(
4
)).

As in Theorem
63
, we have a correspondence theorem for the translations (cf.
[
47
, Lemma 9.5(b) pp. 93-94]
):

Theorem 65

(Combinatory weak equalities translate to
\beta
-equalities)
.

Suppose that
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
.

If
CL_{\theta}\vdash_{w}M=N
then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\gamma}M^{\lambda}=N^{\lambda}
for each of
\gamma=\beta_{0},\beta_{r},\beta
.

Proof 6.18
.

By Proposition
12
, it suffices to prove that if
M\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
then
M^{\lambda}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta_{r}}^{\theta}N^{\lambda}
.

The base case of a direct weak reduction follows from Definition
6.1
and Theorem
26
and Proposition
28
and Definition
6.5
(
2
)-(
3
).

For the inductive step for application on the left suppose that
MP\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}NP
because
M\rightarrow_{w}^{\theta}N
. Then by induction hypothesis
M^{\lambda}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta_{r}}^{\theta}N^{\lambda}
. Then
M^{\lambda}P^{\lambda}\twoheadrightarrow_{\beta_{r}}^{\theta}N^{\lambda}P^{\lambda}
, and we are done by Definition
6.5
(
3
). The case of application on the right is similar.

The following is a proposition that we need in order to round out, in the subsequent results, the treatment of the correspondence. It is in this proposition that we use
\eta
-reductions (cf.
[
47
, Lemma 9.16 p. 98]
). We further note, in the proof, where we lambda abstract over both sides of an equality, so that this proposition is not obviously available in the weak version of the lambda calculus.

Proposition 66

(Result on abstracts of translations of combinatory terms)
.

Suppose that
A,B
are types and
B
is regular. For all terms
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
of
CL_{\theta}
, one has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\gamma\eta}([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{\lambda}=\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{\lambda}}
for each of
\gamma=\beta_{0},\beta_{r},\beta
.

Proof 6.19
.

By Proposition
55
(
1
), we have
\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)v=_{w}^{\theta}M
. By Theorem
65
we have
\big(\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)v\big)^{\lambda}=_{\beta_{r}}^{\theta}M^{\lambda}
. By abstracting over
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
, we have
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)v\big)^{\lambda}}=_{\beta_{r}}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{\lambda}}
. By Definition
6.5
(
1
),(
3
) we have that the left-hand side can be simplified to
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)^{\lambda}v\big)}=_{\beta_{r}}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{\lambda}}
. By Proposition
12
, we have
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{\big(\big([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M}\big)^{\lambda}v\big)}=_{\beta_{0}}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{\lambda}}
. Then by
\eta
, we can further simplify the left-hand side to obtain
([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{\lambda}=_{\beta_{0}\eta}^{\theta}\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{\lambda}}
. The application of
\eta
is legal due to Proposition
55
(
2
) and Proposition
64
, which imply that
v\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA
is not free in
([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{\lambda}
.

The second part of the following forms a converse to Theorem
63
(cf.
[
47
, Theorem 9.17(c)-(d) p. 98]
):

Theorem 67

(Translates of combinatory weak equalities are beta-eta equalities)
.

(1)

For all types
B
and all terms
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, one has
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\gamma\eta}(M^{cl})^{\lambda}=M
for each of
\gamma=\beta_{0},\beta_{r},\beta
.

(2)

For all terms
M,N\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptA

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, if
CL_{\theta}\vdash_{w}M^{cl}=N^{cl}
then
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\gamma\eta}M=N
for each of
\gamma=\beta_{0},\beta_{r},\beta
.

Proof 6.20
.

For (
1
), this is by an induction on complexity of
M\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394ptB
.

For variables and constants, from Definition
6.4
(
1
) followed by Definition
6.5
(
1
), one has that
(M^{cl})^{\lambda}
is
M
itself.

For application, from Definition
6.4
(
2
) followed by Definition
6.5
(
3
), one has that
((M_{0}M_{1})^{cl})^{\lambda}
is
(M_{0}^{cl})^{\lambda}(M_{1}^{cl})^{\lambda}
, and then we are done by induction hypothesis.

For lambda abstraction, from Definition
6.4
(
3
), we have that
(\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M})^{cl}
is
[{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{cl}}
. By Proposition
66
, since
M^{cl}
is a term of
CL_{\theta}
, we have that
([{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}]\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{M^{cl}})^{\lambda}
is
\gamma\eta
-equivalent to
\lambda{v}\hskip-2.13394pt:\hskip-2.13394pt{A}\hskip 0.99585pt.\hskip 0.99585pt{(M^{cl})^{\lambda}}
in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}
, and we are done by induction hypothesis.

For (
2
), suppose
CL_{\theta}\vdash_{w}M^{cl}=N^{cl}
. By Theorem
65
we have that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\gamma}(M^{cl})^{\lambda}=(N^{cl})^{\lambda}
. Then by (
1
) we have that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\gamma\eta}M=N
.

6.6.
Conservation and the weak lambda calculus

We are finally in a position to prove our partial conservation result:

See
7

Proof 6.21
.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\omega}\vdash_{w\beta}M=N
. By Theorem
63
we have
CL\vdash_{w}M^{cl}=N^{cl}
. Since
M^{cl},N^{cl}
are terms of
CL_{\theta}
, we have by Corollary
54
that
CL_{\theta}\vdash_{w}M^{cl}=N^{cl}
. By Theorem
67
(
2
) we have that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\theta}\vdash_{\beta\eta}M=N
.
